**England** is a [country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Countries_of_the_United_Kingdom) that is part of the [United Kingdom](31717.md). It is located on the island of [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Britain), of which it covers about 62%, and [more than 100 smaller adjacent islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_islands_of_England). England shares [a land border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Scottish_border) with [Scotland](26994.md) to the north and [another land border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/England%E2%80%93Wales_border) with [Wales](69894.md) to the west, and is surrounded by the [North Sea](21179.md) to the east, the [English Channel](9230.md) to the south, the [Celtic Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_Sea) to the south-west, and the [Irish Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Sea) to the west. [Continental Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Europe) lies to the south-east, and [Ireland](147575.md) to the west. The population was 56,490,048 according to the [2021 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_Kingdom_census), with nine million inhabitants in its [largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_urban_areas_in_the_United_Kingdom), [London](17867.md).&#x20;

The area now called England was first inhabited by modern humans during the [Upper Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Paleolithic). It takes its name from the [Angles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angles_\(tribe\)), a [Germanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic_peoples) tribe who settled during the 5th and 6th centuries. England became a unified state in the 10th century and has had extensive [cultural and legal impact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicisation) on the wider world since the [Age of Discovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Discovery), which began during the 15th century.[^cite_note-15] The [Kingdom of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_England), which included Wales after 1535, ceased to be a separate [sovereign state](23604120.md) on 1 May 1707, when the [Acts of Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_of_Union_1707) brought into effect a political union with the [Kingdom of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Scotland), forming the [Kingdom of Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain).[^cite_note-16]&#x20;

England is the origin of the [English language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language), the [English legal system](85441.md) (which served as the basis for the [common law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_law) systems of many other countries), [association football](10568.md), and the [Anglican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicanism) branch of Christianity; [its parliamentary system of government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westminster_system) has been widely adopted by other nations.[^cite_note-17] The [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution) began in 18th-century England, transforming its society into the world's first industrialised nation.[^cite_note-18] England is home to the two [oldest universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_oldest_universities_in_continuous_operation) in the [English-speaking world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English-speaking_world): the [University of Oxford](31797.md), founded in 1096, and the [University of Cambridge](25978572.md), founded in 1209.&#x20;

England's terrain chiefly consists of low hills and [plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plain), especially in the [centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midlands) and [south](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_England). Upland and mountainous terrain is mostly found in the [north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_England) and west, including [Dartmoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dartmoor), the [Lake District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_District), the [Pennines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennines), and the [Shropshire Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shropshire_Hills). The [London metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_metropolitan_area) has a population of over 15 million as of 2025, representing the United Kingdom's largest metropolitan area. England's population of 56.3 million comprises 84% of the population of the United Kingdom, largely concentrated around London, the [South East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_East_England), and conurbations in the [Midlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midlands), the [North West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_West_England), the [North East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_East_England), and [Yorkshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yorkshire), which each developed as major industrial regions during the 19th century.[^cite_note-2011_census-19]&#x20;

## Toponymy

See also: [Toponymy of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toponymy_of_England)

The name "England" is derived from the [Old English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_English) name *Englaland*, which means "land of the [Angles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angles_\(tribe\))".[^cite_note-20] The Angles were one of the [Germanic tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic_peoples) that settled in Great Britain during the [Early Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Middle_Ages). They came from the [Angeln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angeln) region of what is now the German state of [Schleswig-Holstein](27126.md).[^cite_note-21] The earliest recorded use of the term, as "*Engla londe*", is in the late-ninth-century translation into Old English of [Bede](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bede)'s *[Ecclesiastical History of the English People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecclesiastical_History_of_the_English_People)*. The term was then used to mean "the land inhabited by the English", and it included English people in what is now south-east Scotland but was then part of the English kingdom of [Northumbria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northumbria). The *[Anglo-Saxon Chronicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Saxon_Chronicle)* recorded that the [Domesday Book](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domesday_Book) of 1086 covered the whole of England, meaning the English kingdom, but a few years later the *Chronicle* stated that King [Malcolm III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_III) went "out of Scotlande into [Lothian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lothian) in Englaland", thus using it in the more ancient sense.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMolyneaux20156–7-22]&#x20;

The earliest attested reference to the Angles occurs in the 1st-century work by [Tacitus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tacitus), *[Germania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germania_\(book\))*, in which the [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin) word *Anglii* is used.[^cite_note-Fordham-23] The etymology of the tribal name itself is disputed by scholars; it has been suggested that it derives from the shape of the Angeln peninsula, an *angular* shape.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECresswell201016-24] How and why a term derived from the name of this tribe, rather than others such as the [Saxons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saxons), came to be used for the entire country is not known, but it seems this is related to the custom of calling the Germanic people in Britain *Angli Saxones* or English Saxons to distinguish them from continental Saxons (Eald-Seaxe) of Old Saxony in [Germany](11867.md).[^cite_note-25] In [Scottish Gaelic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Gaelic), the Saxon tribe gave their name to the word for England (*Sasunn*);[^cite_note-26] similarly, the [Welsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_language) name for the English language is *Saesneg*. A romantic name for England is [Loegria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lloegyr), related to the Welsh word for England, *Lloegr*, and made popular by its use in [Arthurian legend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthurian_legend). *[Albion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albion)* is also applied to England in a more poetic capacity,[^cite_note-27] though its original meaning is the island of Britain as a whole.&#x20;

## History

Main article: [History of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_England)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of English history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_English_history).

### Prehistory

Main article: [Prehistoric Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistoric_Britain)

Sun shining through row of upright standing stones with other stones horizontally on the top.

[Stonehenge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonehenge), a [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) monument



The [Battersea Shield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battersea_Shield) is one of the most significant pieces of ancient [Celtic art](2082902.md) found in Britain.

The earliest known evidence of human presence in what is now England was that of *[Homo antecessor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_antecessor)*, dating to about 780,000 years ago. The oldest [proto-human](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-human) bones discovered in England date from 500,000 years ago.[^cite_note-28] Modern humans are known to have inhabited the area during the [Upper Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Paleolithic) period, though permanent settlements were only established within the last 6,000 years.[^cite_note-29] After the [last ice age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_glacial_period) only large mammals such as [mammoths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mammoth), [bison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bison) and [woolly rhinoceros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woolly_rhinoceros) remained. Roughly 11,000 years ago, when the [ice sheets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_sheets) began to recede, humans repopulated the area; genetic research suggests they came from the northern part of the [Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOppenheimer2006173-30] The sea level was lower than the present day and Britain was connected by [land bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_bridge) to Ireland and [Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia).[^cite_note-31] As the seas rose, it was separated from Ireland 10,000 years ago and from [Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia) two millennia later. [Neolithic farmers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_European_Farmers) from the Iberian Peninsula [migrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-modern_human_migration) to the region around 4100 BC.[^cite_note-32]&#x20;

The [Beaker culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaker_culture) arrived around 2,500 BC, introducing drinking and food vessels constructed from clay, as well as vessels used as reduction pots to smelt copper ores.[^cite_note-33] It was during this time that major [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) monuments such as [Stonehenge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonehenge) (phase III) and [Avebury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avebury) were constructed. By heating together tin and copper, which were in abundance in the area, the Beaker culture people made [bronze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze), and later iron from iron ores. The development of iron [smelting](28716.md) allowed the construction of better [ploughs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plough), advancing agriculture (for instance, with [Celtic fields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_field)), as well as the production of more effective weapons.[^cite_note-34]&#x20;

During the [Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Iron_Age), [Celtic culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celts), deriving from the [Hallstatt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hallstatt_culture) and [La Tène cultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_T%C3%A8ne_culture), arrived from Central Europe. [Brythonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_language_\(Celtic\)) was the spoken language during this time. Society was tribal; according to [Ptolemy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemy)'s *[Geographia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geographia)* there were around 20 tribes in the area. Like other regions on the edge of the Empire, Britain had long enjoyed trading links with the Romans. Julius Caesar of the [Roman Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Republic) attempted to [invade twice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesar%27s_invasions_of_Britain) in 55 BC; although largely unsuccessful, he managed to set up a [client king](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_client_kingdoms_in_Britain) from the [Trinovantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinovantes).&#x20;

### Ancient history



[Housesteads Roman fort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housesteads_Roman_Fort), Northumberland, the northern limit of Roman England

The Romans invaded Britain in 43 AD during the reign of Emperor [Claudius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claudius), subsequently [conquering much of Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_conquest_of_Britain), and the area was incorporated into the Roman Empire as [Britannia province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Britain).[^cite_note-35] The best-known of the native tribes who attempted to resist were the [Catuvellauni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catuvellauni) led by [Caratacus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caratacus). Later, an uprising led by [Boudica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boudica), Queen of the [Iceni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iceni), ended with Boudica's suicide following her defeat at the [Battle of Watling Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defeat_of_Boudica).[^cite_note-36] The author of one study of Roman Britain suggested that from 43 AD to 84 AD, the Roman invaders killed somewhere between 100,000 and 250,000 people from a population of perhaps 2,000,000.[^cite_note-37] This era saw a [Greco-Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Roman) culture prevail with the introduction of [Roman law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_law), [Roman architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_architecture), [aqueducts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_aqueducts_in_the_Roman_Empire), [sewers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanitation_in_ancient_Rome), agricultural items and silk.[^cite_note-38] In the early 3rd century, Emperor [Septimius Severus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Septimius_Severus) died at [Eboracum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eboracum) (present-day [York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/York)), where [Constantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_the_Great) was proclaimed emperor in 306 AD.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERankov199416-39]&#x20;

There is debate about when Christianity was first introduced; it was no later than the 4th century, probably much earlier. According to [Bede](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bede), missionaries were sent from Rome by [Eleutherius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Eleuterus) at the request of the chieftain [Lucius of Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucius_of_Britain) in 180 AD, to settle differences as to Eastern and Western ceremonials, which were disturbing the church. There are traditions linked to Glastonbury claiming an introduction through [Joseph of Arimathea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_of_Arimathea), while others claim through Lucius of Britain.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWright2008143-40] By 410, during the [decline of the Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decline_of_the_Roman_Empire), Britain was left exposed by the [end of Roman rule in Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/End_of_Roman_rule_in_Britain) and the withdrawal of Roman army units, to defend the frontiers in continental Europe and partake in civil wars.[^cite_note-james_anglosaxons-41] Celtic Christian monastic and missionary movements flourished. This period of Christianity was influenced by ancient Celtic culture in its sensibilities, polity, practices and theology. Local "congregations" were centred in the monastic community and monastic leaders were more like chieftains, as peers, rather than in the more hierarchical system of the Roman-dominated church.[^cite_note-Lehane-42]&#x20;

### Middle Ages

Main article: [England in the Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/England_in_the_Middle_Ages)

Studded and decorated metallic mask of human face.

Replica of the 7th-century ceremonial [Sutton Hoo helmet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sutton_Hoo_helmet) from the [Kingdom of East Anglia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_East_Anglia)

[Roman military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_of_ancient_Rome) withdrawals left Britain open to invasion by pagan, seafaring warriors from north-western continental [Europe](9239.md), chiefly the Saxons, [Angles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angles_\(tribe\)), [Jutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jutes) and Frisians who had long raided the coasts of the Roman province. These groups then began to settle in increasing numbers over the course of the fifth and sixth centuries, initially in the eastern part of the country.[^cite_note-james_anglosaxons-41] Their advance was contained for some decades after the Britons' victory at the [Battle of Mount Badon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Badon), but subsequently resumed, overrunning the fertile lowlands of Britain and reducing the area under [Brittonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Britons_\(historical\)) control to a series of separate enclaves in the more rugged country to the west by the end of the 6th century. Contemporary texts describing this period are extremely scarce, giving rise to its description as a [Dark Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dark_Ages_\(historiography\)). Details of the [Anglo-Saxon settlement of Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Saxon_settlement_of_Britain) are consequently subject to considerable disagreement; the emerging consensus is that it occurred on a large scale in the south and east but was less substantial to the north and west, where Celtic languages continued to be spoken even in areas under Anglo-Saxon control.[^cite_note-43][^cite_note-Härke,_Heinrich_2011-44] Roman-dominated Christianity had, in general, been replaced in the conquered territories by [Anglo-Saxon paganism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Saxon_paganism), but was [reintroduced by missionaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianisation_of_Anglo-Saxon_England) from Rome led by [Augustine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augustine_of_Canterbury) from 597.[^cite_note-45] Disputes between the Roman- and Celtic-dominated forms of Christianity ended in victory for the Roman tradition at the [Council of Whitby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Whitby) (664), which was ostensibly about [tonsures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tonsure) (clerical haircuts) and the date of Easter, but more significantly, about the differences in Roman and Celtic forms of authority, theology, and practice.[^cite_note-Lehane-42]&#x20;

During the settlement period the lands ruled by the incomers seem to have been fragmented into numerous tribal territories, but by the 7th century, when substantial evidence of the situation again becomes available, these had coalesced into roughly a dozen kingdoms including [Northumbria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northumbria), [Mercia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercia), [Wessex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wessex), [East Anglia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_East_Anglia), [Essex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Essex), [Kent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kent) and [Sussex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Sussex). Over the following centuries, this process of political consolidation continued.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKirby20004-46] The 7th century saw a struggle for hegemony between Northumbria and Mercia, which in the 8th century gave way to Mercian preeminence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELyon196023-47] In the early 9th century Mercia was displaced as the foremost kingdom by Wessex. Later in that century escalating attacks by the [Danes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danes_\(Germanic_tribe\)) culminated in the conquest of the north and east of England, overthrowing the kingdoms of Northumbria, Mercia and East Anglia. Wessex under [Alfred the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_the_Great) was left as the only surviving English kingdom, and under his successors, it steadily expanded at the expense of the kingdoms of the [Danelaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danelaw). This brought about the political unification of England, first accomplished under [Æthelstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%86thelstan) in 927 and definitively established after further conflicts by [Eadred](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eadred) in 953. A fresh wave of Scandinavian attacks from the late 10th century ended with the conquest of this united kingdom by [Sweyn Forkbeard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweyn_Forkbeard) in 1013 and again by his son [Cnut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cnut_the_Great) in 1016, turning it into the centre of a short-lived [North Sea Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Sea_Empire) that also included [Denmark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Denmark) and [Norway](21241.md). However, the native royal dynasty was restored with the accession of [Edward the Confessor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_the_Confessor) in 1042.&#x20;

King Henry V at the Battle of Agincourt, 1415.

[King Henry V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Henry_V_of_England) at the [Battle of Agincourt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Agincourt), fought on [Saint Crispin's Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Crispin%27s_Day) and concluded with an English victory against a larger French army in the [Hundred Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hundred_Years%27_War)

Following the death of Edward in January 1066, a disputed succession led to a two-front struggle in which [King Harold Godwinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harold_Godwinson)'s late September victory [at Stamford Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Stamford_Bridge) against a Norwegian invasion army led by [Harald Hardrada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harald_Hardrada) was followed almost immediately by his defeat [at Hastings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Hastings) by [Duke William of Normandy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_the_Conqueror) on 14 October, securing a successful [Norman Conquest of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_Conquest).[^cite_note-48] The [Normans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normans) themselves originated from [Scandinavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scandinavia) and had settled in Normandy in the late 9th and early 10th centuries.[^cite_note-49] This conquest led to the almost total dispossession of the English elite and its replacement by a new French-speaking aristocracy, whose speech had a profound and permanent effect on the English language.[^cite_note-50]&#x20;

Subsequently, the [House of Plantagenet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Plantagenet) from [Anjou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchy_of_Anjou) inherited the English throne under [Henry II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_II_of_England), adding England to the budding [Angevin Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angevin_Empire) of fiefs the family had inherited in France including [Aquitaine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchy_of_Aquitaine).[^cite_note-Bartlett_p124-51] They reigned for three centuries, some noted monarchs being [Richard I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_I_of_England), [Edward I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_I_of_England), [Edward III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_III) and [Henry V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_V_of_England).[^cite_note-Bartlett_p124-51] The period saw changes in trade and legislation, including the signing of [Magna Carta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magna_Carta), an English legal charter used to limit the sovereign's powers by law and protect the privileges of freemen. Catholic [monasticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monasticism) flourished, providing philosophers, and the universities of Oxford and Cambridge were founded with royal patronage. The [Principality of Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Wales) became a Plantagenet fief during the 13th century[^cite_note-52] and the [Lordship of Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lordship_of_Ireland) was given to the English monarchy by the Pope. During the 14th century, the Plantagenets and the [House of Valois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Valois) claimed to be legitimate claimants to the [House of Capet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Capet) and of France; the two powers clashed in the [Hundred Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hundred_Years%27_War).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFowler1967208-53] The [Black Death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Death) epidemic [hit England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Death_in_England); starting in 1348, it eventually killed up to half of England's [inhabitants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_demography).[^cite_note-54]&#x20;

Between 1453 and 1487, a civil war known as the [War of the Roses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Roses) waged between the two branches of the royal family, the [Yorkists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_York) and [Lancastrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Lancaster).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrofton2007111-55] The Yorkists eventually lost the throne to the [Tudors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Tudor), a branch of the Lancastrians headed by [Henry Tudor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_VII_of_England) who invaded with Welsh and Breton mercenaries, gaining victory at the [Battle of Bosworth Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bosworth_Field) where the Yorkist king [Richard III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_III_of_England) was killed.[^cite_note-56]&#x20;

### Early modern period



[King Henry VIII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Henry_VIII) (1491–1547)



[Queen Elizabeth I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Elizabeth_I) (1533–1603)

During the [Tudor period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tudor_period), England began to develop a [standing navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Navy), and exploration intensified in the [Age of Discovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Discovery).[^cite_note-57] [Henry VIII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_VIII) broke from communion with the Catholic Church, over issues relating to his divorce, under the [Acts of Supremacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_of_Supremacy) in 1534 which proclaimed the monarch head of the [Church of England](5955.md). In contrast with much of European [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism), the [roots of the split](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Reformation) were more political than theological.[^cite_note-59] He also legally incorporated his ancestral land Wales into the Kingdom of England with the [1535–1542 acts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laws_in_Wales_Acts_1535%E2%80%931542). There were internal religious conflicts during the reigns of Henry's daughters, [Mary I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_I_of_England) and [Elizabeth I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_I). The former took the country back to Catholicism while the latter broke from it again, forcefully asserting the supremacy of [Anglicanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicanism). The [Elizabethan era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabethan_era) is the epoch in the Tudor age of the reign of Queen Elizabeth I ("the Virgin Queen"). Historians often depict it as the [golden age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_age_\(metaphor\)) in English history that represented the apogee of the English Renaissance and saw the flowering of great art, drama, poetry, music and literature.[^cite_note-60] England during this period had a centralised, well-organised, and effective government.[^cite_note-61]&#x20;

Competing with [Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Empire), the first English colony in the Americas was founded in 1585 by explorer [Walter Raleigh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Raleigh) in [Virginia](32432.md) and named [Roanoke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roanoke_Colony). The Roanoke colony failed and is known as the lost colony after it was found abandoned on the return of the late-arriving supply ship.[^cite_note-62] With the [East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company), England also competed with the [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Empire) and [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_colonial_empire) in the East. During the Elizabethan period, England was at war with Spain. An [armada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Armada) sailed from Spain in 1588 as part of a wider plan to invade England and re-establish a Catholic monarchy. This plan was thwarted by bad coordination, stormy weather and successful harrying attacks by an English fleet under [Lord Howard of Effingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Howard,_1st_Earl_of_Nottingham). The failure of the 1588 expedition did not deter Spain, which launched two further armadas in [1596](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2nd_Spanish_Armada) and [1597](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3rd_Spanish_Armada); however, these subsequent fleets were likewise dispersed by severe Atlantic storms.&#x20;

### Union with Scotland and English Civil War

Further information: [Union of the Crowns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_the_Crowns) and [Treaty of Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Union)



[King of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Scotland), [James VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_James_VI_of_Scotland), became [King of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_England) as James I in 1603, forming the [Union of the Crowns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_the_Crowns)

The political structure of the island changed in 1603, when [James VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_VI_of_Scotland), [King of Scots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Scots), inherited the English throne as James I, thereby creating a [personal union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_the_Crowns) between the two rival realms.[^cite_note-Britons-63] He styled himself [King of Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Great_Britain), although this had no basis in English law.[^cite_note-64] Under the auspices of James VI and I the Authorised [King James Version](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_James_Version) of the Holy Bible was published in 1611. It was the standard version of the Bible read by most Protestant Christians for four hundred years until modern revisions were produced in the 20th century.&#x20;

Based on conflicting political, religious and social positions, the [English Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Civil_War) was fought between the supporters of [Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_Parliament) and those of King [Charles I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_I_of_England), known colloquially as [Roundheads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roundhead) and [Cavaliers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cavalier) respectively. This was an interwoven part of the wider multifaceted [Wars of the Three Kingdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wars_of_the_Three_Kingdoms), involving [Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Scotland) and [Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Ireland). The Parliamentarians were victorious, [Charles I was executed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Execution_of_Charles_I) and the kingdom replaced by the [Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_England). Leader of the Parliament forces, [Oliver Cromwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oliver_Cromwell) declared himself [Lord Protector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Protector) in 1653; a period of [personal rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Protectorate) followed.[^cite_note-O_Cromwell-65] After Cromwell's death and the resignation of his son [Richard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Cromwell) as Lord Protector, [Charles II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_II_of_England) was invited to return as monarch in 1660, in a move called the [Restoration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Restoration). With the reopening of theatres, fine arts, literature and performing arts flourished throughout the Restoration of the "Merry Monarch" Charles II.[^cite_note-66] After the [Glorious Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glorious_Revolution) of 1688, it was constitutionally established that King and Parliament should rule together, though [Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament) would have the real power. This was established with the [Bill of Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_of_Rights_1689) in 1689. Amongst the statutes set down were that the law could only be made by Parliament and could not be suspended by the King, also that the King could not impose taxes or raise an army without the prior approval of Parliament.[^cite_note-67] Also since that time, no British monarch has entered the [House of Commons](13828.md) when it is sitting, which is annually commemorated at the [State Opening of Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Opening_of_Parliament) by the British monarch when the doors of the House of Commons are slammed in the face of the monarch's messenger, symbolising the rights of Parliament and its independence from the monarch.[^cite_note-68] With the founding of the [Royal Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Society) in 1660, science was greatly encouraged.&#x20;

Painting of seated male figure, with long black hair wearing a white cape and breeches.

The [English Restoration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Restoration) restored the monarchy under King [Charles II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_II_of_England) and peace after the [English Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Civil_War).

In 1666 the [Great Fire of London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Fire_of_London) gutted the city of London, but it was rebuilt shortly afterward with many significant buildings designed by Sir [Christopher Wren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Wren).[^cite_note-69] By the mid-to-late 17th century, two political factions had emerged – the [Tories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tory) and [Whigs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whigs_\(British_political_party\)). Though the Tories initially supported Catholic king [James II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_II_of_England), some of them, along with the Whigs, during the [Revolution of 1688](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glorious_Revolution) invited the Dutch [Prince William of Orange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_III_of_England) to defeat James and become the king. Some English people, primarily in the north, were [Jacobites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobitism) and continued to support James and his sons. Under the [Stuart dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Stuart) England expanded in trade, finance and prosperity. The Royal Navy developed Europe's largest merchant fleet.[^cite_note-70] After the parliaments of England and Scotland agreed,[^cite_note-Union_with_Scotland-71] the two countries joined in [political union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_union), to create the [Kingdom of Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain) in 1707.[^cite_note-Britons-63] To accommodate the union, institutions such as the law and national churches of each remained separate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGallagher200614-72]&#x20;

### Late modern and contemporary periods



The [River Thames](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Thames) during the [Georgian period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_period) from the Terrace of Somerset House looking towards St. Paul's, c. 1750

Following the formation of the Kingdom of Great Britain, output from the Royal Society and other [English initiatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Enlightenment) combined with the [Scottish Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Enlightenment) to create innovations in science and engineering, while the enormous growth in [British overseas trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triangular_trade) protected by the [Royal Navy](26061.md) paved the way for the establishment of the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire). Domestically it drove the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution), a period of profound change in the [socioeconomic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socioeconomics) and cultural conditions of England, resulting in industrialised agriculture, manufacture, engineering and mining, as well as new and pioneering road, rail and water networks to facilitate their expansion and development.[^cite_note-73] The opening of Northwest England's [Bridgewater Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bridgewater_Canal) in 1761 ushered in the [canal age in Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_British_canal_system).[^cite_note-Briton20015-74] In 1825, the world's first permanent steam locomotive-hauled [passenger railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passenger_rail_terminology) – the [Stockton and Darlington Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stockton_and_Darlington_Railway) – opened to the public.[^cite_note-Briton20015-74]&#x20;

During the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution), many workers moved from England's countryside to new and expanding urban industrial areas to work in factories, for instance at [Birmingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham) and [Manchester](20206.md),[^cite_note-75] with the latter the world's first industrial city.[^cite_note-Industrial_city-76] England maintained relative stability throughout the [French Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution), under [George III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_III) and [William Pitt the Younger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Pitt_the_Younger). The [regency of George IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regency_era) is noted for its elegance and achievements in the fine arts and architecture.[^cite_note-77] During the [Napoleonic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars), [Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon) planned to [invade from the south-east](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon%27s_planned_invasion_of_the_United_Kingdom); however, this failed to manifest and the Napoleonic forces were defeated by the British: at sea by [Horatio Nelson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horatio_Nelson), and on land by [Arthur Wellesley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington). The victory at the [Battle of Trafalgar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Trafalgar) confirmed the naval supremacy Britain had established during the eighteenth century.[^cite_note-78] The Napoleonic Wars fostered a concept of [Britishness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Britishness) and a united national [British people](19097669.md), shared with the English, Scots and Welsh.[^cite_note-Colley1-79]&#x20;

multi-storey square industrial buildings beyond a river

The [Battle of Trafalgar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Trafalgar) was a naval engagement between the [Royal Navy](26061.md) and the combined fleets of France and Spain during the [Napoleonic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars).

[^cite_note-80]

London became the largest and most populous metropolitan area in the world during the [Victorian era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victorian_era), and trade within the British Empire – as well as the standing of the British military and navy – was prestigious.[^cite_note-81] Technologically, this era saw many innovations that proved key to the United Kingdom's power and prosperity.[^cite_note-Atterbury_2011-82] Political agitation at home from radicals such as the [Chartists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chartism) and the [suffragettes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suffragette) enabled legislative reform and [universal suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_suffrage).[^cite_note-83]&#x20;

Power shifts in east-central Europe led to World War I; hundreds of thousands of English soldiers died fighting for the United Kingdom as part of the [Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I).[^cite_note-85] Two decades later, in [World War II](32927.md), the United Kingdom was again one of the [Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II). Developments in warfare technology saw many cities damaged by air-raids during [the Blitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Blitz). Following the war, the British Empire experienced rapid [decolonisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decolonisation), and there was a speeding-up of technological innovations; automobiles became the primary means of transport and [Frank Whittle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Whittle)'s development of the [jet engine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_engine) led to wider [air travel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_travel).[^cite_note-86] Residential patterns were altered in England by private motoring, and by the creation of the [National Health Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Health_Service_\(England\)) in 1948, providing [publicly funded health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Publicly_funded_health_care) to all permanent residents free at the point of need. Combined, these prompted the reform of [local government in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_England) in the mid-20th century.[^cite_note-87]&#x20;



The [Victorian era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victorian_era) is often cited as a [Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Age). Painting done by [William Powell Frith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Powell_Frith) to show cultural divisions.&#x20;

Since the 20th century, there has been significant population movement to England, mostly from other parts of the [British Isles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Isles), but also from the [Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations), particularly the [Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent).[^cite_note-88] Since the 1970s there has been a large move away from manufacturing and an increasing emphasis on the [service industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Service_industry).[^cite_note-Thatcher-89] As part of the [United Kingdom](31717.md), the area joined a [common market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_market) initiative called the [European Economic Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Economic_Community) which became the [European Union](9317.md).&#x20;

Since the late 20th century, the [administration of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_the_United_Kingdom) has moved towards [devolved governance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution) in Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland.[^cite_note-90] [England and Wales](275187.md) continues to exist as a jurisdiction within the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-BBC_Wales-91] Devolution has stimulated a greater emphasis on a more English-specific identity and patriotism.[^cite_note-92] There is no devolved English government, but an attempt to create a similar system on a sub-regional basis was rejected by [referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_North_East_England_devolution_referendum).[^cite_note-The_Times_2004-11-05-93]&#x20;

## Governance

England is part of the United Kingdom,[^cite_note-ONS_Geography_Guide-94] a [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy) with a [parliamentary system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_system).[^cite_note-95] There has not been a [government of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_England) since 1707, when the [Acts of Union 1707](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_of_Union_1707),[^cite_note-96] putting into effect the terms of the [Treaty of Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Union), joined England and Scotland to form the [Kingdom of Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain).[^cite_note-Union_with_Scotland-71] Before the union England was ruled by [its monarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_English_monarchs) and the [Parliament of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_England).&#x20;

Photograph of rectangular floodlight building, reflected in water. The building has multiple towers including one at each end. The tower on the right includes an illuminated clock face.

The [Palace of Westminster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_Westminster), the seat of the [Parliament of the United Kingdom](13964.md) in which England is directly governed

England is governed directly by the [Parliament of the United Kingdom](13964.md), although other [countries of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Countries_of_the_United_Kingdom) have [devolved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution) governments.[^cite_note-Devolution-97] There has been debate about how to counterbalance this in England. Originally it was planned that various [regions of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_England) would be devolved, but following the proposal's rejection by the [North East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_East_England) in a [2004 referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_North_East_England_devolution_referendum), this has not been carried out.[^cite_note-The_Times_2004-11-05-93] In 2024, an England-only intergovernmental body, known as the [Mayoral Council for England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayoral_Council_for_England), was established to bring together ministers from the [UK Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UK_Government), the [Mayor of London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_London) and the leaders of [combined authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_authority).[^cite_note-98]&#x20;

In 2026 the [English Devolution and Community Empowerment Act 2026](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Devolution_and_Community_Empowerment_Act_2026) received royal assent and established a three tier system of Governance consisting of [National](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governance_of_England), [Strategic Authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_authority) and [Principal Authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principal_area).[^cite_note-\:5-99][^cite_note-\:6-100]&#x20;

### Politics

Main article: [Politics of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_England)

In the [House of Commons](13828.md) which is the [lower house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_house) of the British Parliament based at the [Palace of Westminster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_Westminster), there are 543 members of parliament (MPs) for constituencies in England, out of the 650 total.[^cite_note-101] England is represented by 347 MPs from the [Labour Party](19279158.md), 116 from the [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)), 65 from the [Liberal Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Democrats_\(UK\)), four for [Reform UK](59757202.md) and four for the [Green Party of England and Wales](79787.md).[^cite_note-102]&#x20;

### Tiers of Governance

Main article: [Governance of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governance_of_England)



Tiers of Government in England

There are currently three overarching categories of governance in England – National, [Strategic Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_authority) and [Principal Authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principal_areas).[^cite_note-\:5-99][^cite_note-\:6-100]&#x20;

#### National

England does not have its own [Devolved Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolved_English_parliament), unlike Scotland ([Scottish Parliament](61188.md)), Wales ([Welsh Senedd](238480.md)) and Northern Ireland ([Northern Ireland Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Ireland_Assembly)). Instead the [U.K Parliament](13964.md) creates legislation for England.[^cite_note-\:5-99]&#x20;

#### Strategic Authority

Main articles: [Devolution in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution_in_England) and [Strategic authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_authority)



Diagram of English strategic authorities

*Strategic Authorities* are sub-regional entities consisting of [Combined and County Combined Authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_authority) and the [Greater London Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_London_Authority).[^cite_note-Marc_WH-6]&#x20;

As of 2026 there are currently 18 Combined and County Combined Authorities in England, with 16 of those having a [directly elected mayor](598892.md).[^cite_note-103]&#x20;

From 1994 until the early 2010s England was divided for a few purposes into regions; a [1998 referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Greater_London_Authority_referendum) for the London Region created the [London Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Assembly) two years later.[^cite_note-104] A failed [2004 North East England devolution referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_North_East_England_devolution_referendum) cancelled further [regional assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_Assemblies_in_England) devolution[^cite_note-The_Times_2004-11-05-93] with the regional structure outside London abolished.&#x20;

#### Principal Authorities

Main articles: [Local government in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_England) and [Principal council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principal_council)

*Principal Authorities* consist of [unitary councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_authority), [district councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Districts_of_England) and [county councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_council).[^cite_note-\:6-100] Outside the London region, England's highest tier is the 48 [ceremonial counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceremonial_counties_of_England).[^cite_note-britannicagov-105] These are used primarily as a geographical frame of reference. Of these, 38 have developed gradually since the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages); these were reformed to 51 in 1974 and to their current number in 1996.[^cite_note-maud-106] Each has a [Lord Lieutenant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Lieutenant) and a [High Sheriff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Sheriff); these appointees represent the [British monarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_monarch) locally.[^cite_note-britannicagov-105] Some counties, such as [Herefordshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herefordshire), are only divided further into civil parishes. The royal county of [Berkshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berkshire) and the metropolitan counties have a different status from other ceremonial counties.[^cite_note-107] In 1974, the [Local Government Act 1972](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Government_Act_1972) created a two-tier system in England with 39 non-metropolitan (shire) counties and 6 metropolitan counties. The shire counties were two-tier (county council + district councils), while the metropolitan counties had a different division of functions.[^cite_note-108]&#x20;

The metropolitan county councils were abolished in 1986.[^cite_note-109] Further reforms in the mid-1990s (mainly 1995–1998) replaced two-tier structures with unitary authorities in many areas. The 1996 changes formally separated ceremonial counties (for lieutenancy and ceremonial purposes) from administrative counties (for local government).[^cite_note-\:7-110]&#x20;

This created clearer distinction: ceremonial counties often follow historic or adjusted boundaries, while administrative areas reflect current unitary or two-tier council structures.[^cite_note-\:7-110]&#x20;

Administratively, London comprises 32 [London boroughs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_boroughs) and the [City of London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_London).[^cite_note-111] The 32 London boroughs form the ceremonial county of Greater London, with the City of London being a separate ceremonial county.&#x20;

England is also divided into local government districts.[^cite_note-localgov-112] The district can align to a ceremonial county, or be a district tier within a [shire county](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shire_counties), be a [royal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_borough) or [metropolitan borough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_borough), have [borough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borough_status_in_the_United_Kingdom) or [city status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_status_in_the_United_Kingdom), or be a [unitary authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_authorities_of_England),[^cite_note-113] London for example has 33 [local government districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_districts).&#x20;

At the community level, much of England is divided into [civil parishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_parishes_in_England) with their own [councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parish_councils_in_England);[^cite_note-114] in Greater London only one such parish, [Queen's Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen%27s_Park,_London), exists as of 2014 after they were [abolished in 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Government_Act_1963) until legislation [allowed their recreation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Government_and_Public_Involvement_in_Health_Act_2007) in 2007.&#x20;

### Law

Main article: [English law](85441.md)



The [Royal Courts of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Courts_of_Justice)

The [English law](85441.md) legal system, developed over the centuries, is the basis of [common law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_law)[^cite_note-115] legal systems used in most [Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations) countries[^cite_note-116] and the United States (except [Louisiana](18130.md)). Despite now being part of the United Kingdom, the legal system of the [Courts of England and Wales](240527.md) continued, under the [Treaty of Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Union), as a separate legal system from [the one used in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots_law). The general essence of English law is that it is made by judges sitting in courts, applying their common sense and knowledge of [legal precedent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_precedent) – *stare decisis* – to the facts before them.[^cite_note-117]&#x20;

The court system is headed by the Senior Courts of England and Wales, consisting of the [Court of Appeal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_of_Appeal_of_England_and_Wales), the [High Court of Justice](26569402.md) for civil cases, and the [Crown Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Court) for criminal cases.[^cite_note-118] The [Supreme Court of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_Kingdom) is the highest court for criminal and civil cases in [England and Wales](275187.md). It was created in 2009 after constitutional changes, taking over the [judicial functions of the House of Lords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_functions_of_the_House_of_Lords).[^cite_note-119] A decision of the Supreme Court is binding on every other court in the hierarchy, which must follow its directions.[^cite_note-120]&#x20;

The [secretary of state for justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_state_for_justice) is the minister responsible to Parliament for the [judiciary](5679670.md), the court system and prisons and probation in England.[^cite_note-121] Crime increased between 1981 and 1995 but fell by 42% in the period 1995–2006.[^cite_note-122] The prison population doubled over the same period, giving it one of the [highest incarceration rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_incarceration_rate) in Western Europe at 147 per 100,000.[^cite_note-Crime_BBC-123] [His Majesty's Prison Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/His_Majesty%27s_Prison_Service), reporting to the [Ministry of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Justice_\(United_Kingdom\)), manages most prisons, housing in 2024 around 87,300 prisoners in England and Wales, the most since 1900.[^cite_note-124]&#x20;

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_England)

### Landscape and rivers



The [Malvern Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malvern_Hills) located in the English counties of [Worcestershire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worcestershire) and [Herefordshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herefordshire). The hills have been designated by the Countryside Agency as an [Area of Outstanding Natural Beauty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Area_of_Outstanding_Natural_Beauty).&#x20;

Geographically, England includes the central and southern two-thirds of the island of Great Britain, plus such offshore islands as the [Isle of Wight](15102.md) and the [Isles of Scilly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isles_of_Scilly). It is bordered by two other countries of the United Kingdom: [to the north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo_Scottish_border) by Scotland and [to the west](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wales-England_border) by [Wales](69894.md).&#x20;

England is closer than any other part of mainland Britain to the European continent. It is separated from [France](5843419.md) ([Hauts-de-France](45093681.md)) by a 21-mile (34 km)[^cite_note-Engchannel-125] sea gap, though the two countries are connected by the [Channel Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_Tunnel) near [Folkestone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folkestone).[^cite_note-126] England also has shores on the [Irish Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Sea), [North Sea](21179.md) and Atlantic Ocean.&#x20;

The ports of London, [Liverpool](18081.md), and [Newcastle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newcastle_upon_Tyne) lie on the tidal rivers [Thames](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Thames), [Mersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Mersey) and [Tyne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Tyne), respectively. At 220 miles (350 km), the [Severn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Severn) is the longest river flowing through England.[^cite_note-127] It empties into the [Bristol Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Channel) and is notable for its [Severn Bore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Severn_Bore) (a [tidal bore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_bore)), which can reach 2 metres (6.6 ft) in height.[^cite_note-128] However, the longest river entirely in England is the Thames, which is 215 miles (346 km) in length.[^cite_note-Thames-129] There are many [lakes in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakes_in_England); the largest is [Windermere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Windermere), within the aptly named [Lake District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_District).[^cite_note-metoffice_nw-130]&#x20;

Most of England's landscape consists of low hills and plains, with upland and mountainous terrain in the north and west of the country. The northern uplands include the [Pennines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennines), a chain of uplands dividing east and west, the Lake District mountains in Cumbria, and the [Cheviot Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheviot_Hills), straddling the border between England and Scotland. The highest point in England, at 978 metres (3,209 ft), is [Scafell Pike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scafell_Pike) in the Lake District.[^cite_note-metoffice_nw-130] The [Shropshire Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shropshire_Hills) are near [Wales](69894.md) while [Dartmoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dartmoor) and [Exmoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exmoor) are two upland areas in the south-west of the country. The approximate dividing line between terrain types is often indicated by the [Tees–Exe line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tees%E2%80%93Exe_line).[^cite_note-131]&#x20;



The village of [Glenridding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glenridding) and [Ullswater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ullswater) in [Cumbria](73021.md)

The Pennines, known as the "backbone of England", are the oldest range of mountains in the country, originating from the end of the [Paleozoic Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleozoic_Era) around 300 million years ago.[^cite_note-132] The geological composition is predominantly sedimentary, featuring significant deposits of [sandstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandstone) and [limestone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limestone), alongside substantial coal seams. There are [karst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karst) landscapes in calcite areas such as parts of [Yorkshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yorkshire) and [Derbyshire](71070.md). The Pennine landscape is high [moorland](553383.md) in upland areas, indented by fertile valleys of the region's rivers. They contain two [national parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_parks_in_England), the [Yorkshire Dales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yorkshire_Dales) and the [Peak District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peak_District). In the [West Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Country), Dartmoor and Exmoor of the Southwest Peninsula include upland moorland supported by granite.[^cite_note-133]&#x20;

The [English Lowlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Lowlands_beech_forests) are in the central and southern regions of the country, consisting of green rolling hills, including the [Cotswold Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotswold_Hills), [Chiltern Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiltern_Hills), [North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Downs) and [South Downs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Downs); where they meet the sea they form white rock exposures such as the [cliffs of Dover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cliffs_of_Dover). The English Lowlands also include flat plains such as [Salisbury Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salisbury_Plain), the [Somerset Levels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somerset_Levels), the [South Coast Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Coast_Plain) and [The Fens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fens).&#x20;

### Climate

Main article: [Climate of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_England)

England has a [temperate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_climate) [maritime climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_climate): it is mild with temperatures not much lower than 0 °C (32 °F) in winter and not much higher than 32 °C (90 °F) in summer.[^cite_note-weather-134] The weather is frequently damp and changeable. The coldest months are January and February, the latter particularly on the [English coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_the_United_Kingdom), while July is normally the warmest. Months with mild to warm weather are May, June, September and October.[^cite_note-weather-134] Rainfall is spread fairly evenly throughout the year.&#x20;

Important influences on the climate of England are its proximity to the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md), its northern [latitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latitude) and the warming of the sea by the [Gulf Stream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Stream).[^cite_note-weather-134] Rainfall is higher in the west, and parts of the [Lake District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_District) receive more rain than anywhere else in the country.[^cite_note-weather-134] Since weather records began, the highest temperature recorded was 40.3 °C (104.5 °F) on 19 July 2022 at [Coningsby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coningsby), [Lincolnshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincolnshire),[^cite_note-135] while the lowest was −26.1 °C (−15.0 °F) on 10 January 1982 in [Edgmond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgmond,_Shropshire), Shropshire.[^cite_note-136]&#x20;

### Nature and wildlife

Main article: [Fauna of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauna_of_England)



The [Eurasian wren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_wren), the most numerous bird species in England

[^cite_note-137]

The fauna of England is similar to that of other areas in the [British Isles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Isles) with a wide range of vertebrate and invertebrate life in a diverse range of habitats.[^cite_note-138] [National nature reserves in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_nature_reserves_in_England) are designated by [Natural England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_England) as key places for [wildlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife) and natural features in England. They were established to protect the most significant areas of habitat and of geological formations. NNRs are managed on behalf of the nation, many by Natural England themselves, but also by non-governmental organisations, including the members of [The Wildlife Trusts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wildlife_Trusts) partnership, the [National Trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Trust_for_Places_of_Historic_Interest_or_Natural_Beauty), and the [Royal Society for the Protection of Birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Society_for_the_Protection_of_Birds). There are 221 NNRs in England covering 110,000 hectares (1,100 square kilometres). Often they contain rare species or nationally important populations of plants and animals.[^cite_note-139] . The [Environment Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environment_Agency) is a non-departmental public body, established in 1995 and sponsored by the [Department for Environment, Food and Rural Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_for_Environment,_Food_and_Rural_Affairs) with responsibilities relating to the protection and enhancement of the environment in England.[^cite_note-140] The [secretary of state for environment, food and rural affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_state_for_environment,_food_and_rural_affairs) is the minister responsible for environmental protection, agriculture, fisheries and rural communities in England.[^cite_note-141]&#x20;



[Red deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_deer) in [Richmond Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richmond_Park). The park was created by [Charles I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_I_of_England) in the 17th century as a [deer park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deer_park_\(England\)).

[^cite_note-Pastscape-142]

England has a [temperate oceanic climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate) in most areas, lacking extremes of cold or heat, but does have a few small areas of [subarctic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subarctic_climate) and warmer areas in [the South West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_West_England). Towards the [North of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_England) the climate becomes colder and most of England's mountains and high hills are located here and have a major impact on the climate and thus the local fauna of the areas. [Deciduous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deciduous) woodlands are common across all of England and provide a great habitat for much of England's wildlife, but these give way in northern and upland areas of England to [coniferous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coniferous) forests (mainly plantations) which also benefit certain forms of wildlife. Some species have adapted to the expanded urban environment, particularly the [red fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_fox), which is the most successful [urban mammal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_wildlife) after the [brown rat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_rat), and other animals such as the [common wood pigeon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_wood_pigeon), both of which thrive in urban and suburban areas.[^cite_note-143]&#x20;

### Major conurbations

See also: [List of places in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_places_in_England)

The [Greater London Built-up Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_London_Built-up_Area) is by far the largest urban area in England[^cite_note-BUA-144] and one of the busiest cities in the world. It is considered a [global city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_city) and has a population larger than any other country in the [United Kingdom](31717.md) besides England itself.[^cite_note-BUA-144] Other urban areas of considerable size and influence tend to be in Northern England or the [English Midlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Midlands).[^cite_note-BUA-144] There are [50 settlements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_the_United_Kingdom) which have designated [city status in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_status_in_the_United_Kingdom), while the wider [United Kingdom](31717.md) has 66.&#x20;

While many cities in England are quite large, such as [Birmingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham), [Sheffield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheffield), [Manchester](20206.md), [Liverpool](18081.md), [Leeds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Leeds), [Newcastle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newcastle_upon_Tyne), [Bradford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Bradford), [Nottingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nottingham), population size is not a prerequisite for city status.[^cite_note-cathcities-145] Traditionally the status was given to towns with [diocesan cathedrals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cathedrals_in_England), so there are smaller cities like [Wells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wells,_Somerset), [Ely](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ely,_Cambridgeshire), [Ripon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ripon), [Truro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truro) and [Chichester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chichester).&#x20;

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_England)



[London](17867.md) is the financial capital of England and the United Kingdom.

England's economy is one of the largest and most dynamic in the world, with a [GDP per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDP_per_capita) of £37,852 in 2022.[^cite_note-ONS_GDP-13] [HM Treasury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HM_Treasury), led by the [chancellor of the exchequer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_exchequer), is responsible for developing and executing the government's [public finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_finance) policy and [economic policy](210522.md).[^cite_note-146] Usually regarded as a [mixed market economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Saxon_economy), it has adopted many [free market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_market) principles, yet maintains an advanced [social welfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_welfare) infrastructure.[^cite_note-147]&#x20;

The economy of England is the largest part of the [UK's economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_United_Kingdom).[^cite_note-regionalacc-148] England is a leader in the chemical and pharmaceutical sectors and in key technical industries, particularly [aerospace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerospace), the [arms industry](608296.md), and the [software industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Software_industry). London, home to the [London Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Stock_Exchange), the United Kingdom's main [stock exchange](28168.md) and the largest in Europe, is England's financial centre, with 100 of Europe's 500 largest corporations being based there.[^cite_note-149] As of 2025, London is the largest financial centre in Europe and the second largest in the world.[^cite_note-150]&#x20;

London has been named as the fastest growing technology hub in Europe, with England having over 100 unique tech companies with a value of \$1 billion or more.[^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152] The [Bank of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_England), founded in 1694 as private banker to the government of England and a [state-owned institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalisation) since 1946, is the United Kingdom's [central bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_bank).[^cite_note-153] The bank has a monopoly on the issue of banknotes in [England and Wales](275187.md), although not in other parts of the UK. The government has devolved responsibility to the bank's [Monetary Policy Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monetary_Policy_Committee_\(United_Kingdom\)) for managing the monetary policy of the country and setting interest rates.[^cite_note-154]&#x20;

A grey coloured convertible.

[Aston Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aston_Martin) manufacture luxury vehicles in England.

England is highly industrialised, but since the 1970s there has been a decline in traditional heavy and manufacturing industries, and an increasing emphasis on a more [service industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Service_industry)-oriented economy.[^cite_note-Thatcher-89] Tourism has become a significant industry, attracting millions of visitors to England each year. The export part of the economy is dominated by [pharmaceuticals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmaceuticals), automotives, [crude oil](23195.md) and petroleum from the English parts of [North Sea oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Sea_oil) along with [Wytch Farm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wytch_Farm), [aircraft engines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aircraft_engine) and alcoholic beverages.[^cite_note-155] The [creative industries](674483.md) accounted for 7 per cent GVA in 2005 and grew at an average of 6 per cent per annum between 1997 and 2005.[^cite_note-156]&#x20;

Agriculture is intensive, highly mechanised and efficient by European standards, producing 60% of food needs with only 2% of the labour force.[^cite_note-157] Two-thirds of production is devoted to livestock, the remainder to arable crops.[^cite_note-158] The main crops that are grown are [wheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheat), [barley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barley), [oats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oat), [potatoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potato), and [sugar beets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugar_beet). England retains a significant fishing industry. Its fleets bring home a variety of fish, ranging from [sole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sole_\(fish\)) to [herring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herring). England is also rich in natural resources including [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal), [petroleum](23195.md), [natural gas](22131.md), [tin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tin), [limestone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limestone), [iron ore](145352.md), [salt](1605200.md), [clay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clay), [chalk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalk), [gypsum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gypsum), [lead](17747.md), and silica.[^cite_note-159]&#x20;

### Science and technology

Main articles: [List of English inventions and discoveries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_English_inventions_and_discoveries) and [Royal Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Society)

Prominent English figures from the field of science and mathematics include Sir [Isaac Newton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Newton), [Charles Darwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Darwin), [Robert Hooke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Hooke), [Alan Turing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Turing), [Stephen Hawking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Hawking), [Edward Jenner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Jenner), [Francis Crick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Crick), [Joseph Lister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Lister,_1st_Baron_Lister), [Joseph Priestley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Priestley), [Thomas Young](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Young_\(scientist\)), [Christopher Wren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Wren) and [Richard Dawkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Dawkins).&#x20;

Torso of man with long white hair and dark coloured jacket

Sir [Isaac Newton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Newton) is one of the most influential figures in the [history of science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_science).

England was a leading centre of the [Scientific Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_Revolution) from the 17th century.[^cite_note-160] As the birthplace of the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution), England was home to many significant inventors during the late 18th and early 19th centuries. Famous English engineers include [Isambard Kingdom Brunel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isambard_Kingdom_Brunel), best known for the creation of the [Great Western Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Western_Railway), a series of famous [steamships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steamship), and numerous important bridges, revolutionising public transport and modern-day engineering.[^cite_note-161] [Thomas Newcomen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Newcomen)'s [steam engine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newcomen_steam_engine) helped spawn the Industrial Revolution.[^cite_note-162]&#x20;

The Father of Railways, [George Stephenson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Stephenson), built the first public inter-city railway line in the world, the [Liverpool and Manchester Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_and_Manchester_Railway), which opened in 1830. With his role in the marketing and manufacturing of the steam engine, and invention of modern coinage, [Matthew Boulton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_Boulton) (business partner of [James Watt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Watt)) is regarded as one of the most influential entrepreneurs in history.[^cite_note-163] The physician [Edward Jenner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Jenner)'s [smallpox vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smallpox_vaccine) is said to have "saved more lives ... than were lost in all the wars of mankind since the beginning of recorded history."[^cite_note-164]&#x20;

Inventions and discoveries of the English include the [jet engine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_engine); the first industrial [spinning machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spinning_frame); [the first computer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Analytical_engine) and the first [modern computer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchester_Baby); the [World Wide Web](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Wide_Web) along with [HTML](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HTML); the first successful human [blood transfusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_transfusion); the motorised [vacuum cleaner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vacuum_cleaner);[^cite_note-americanheritage-165] the [lawn mower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawn_mower); the [seat belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seat_belt); the [hovercraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hovercraft); the [electric motor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_motor); [steam engines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steam_engine); and theories such as the Darwinian theory of [evolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution) and [atomic theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_theory).
