Newton developed the ideas of [universal gravitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_gravitation), [Newtonian mechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newtonian_mechanics), and [calculus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calculus), and [Robert Hooke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Hooke) his eponymously named [law of elasticity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hooke%27s_law_of_elasticity). Other inventions include the iron plate railway, the [thermosiphon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermosiphon), [tarmac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarmacadam), the [rubber band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubber_band), the [mousetrap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mousetrap), ["cat's eye"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cat%27s_eye_\(road\)) [road marker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raised_pavement_marker), joint development of the [light bulb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_bulb), steam [locomotives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Locomotive), the modern [seed drill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seed_drill) and many modern techniques and technologies used in [precision engineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precision_engineering).[^cite_note-invent-166]&#x20;

English innovators made crucial contributions to computing from the earliest concepts to the microchip era. [Charles Babbage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Babbage)[^cite_note-167] and [Ada Lovelace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ada_Lovelace)[^cite_note-168] conceived the first programmable computer, while [George Boole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Boole) created the binary logic underlying all digital systems, known as [boolean logic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boolean_logic).[^cite_note-169] [Alan Turing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Turing) defined the foundations of computing and pioneered artificial intelligence,[^cite_note-170] while [Tommy Flowers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tommy_Flowers) built [Colossus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colossus_computer), proving electronic computing feasible.[^cite_note-171] [Sir Frederic Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederic_C._Williams), [Tom Kilburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Kilburn) and [Geoff Tootill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoff_Tootill) developed the [Manchester Baby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchester_Baby),[^cite_note-172] while [Sir Maurice Wilkes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Wilkes) developed [EDSAC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EDSAC)[^cite_note-173] contributing to the first stored-program computers that established modern computer architecture. [Geoffrey Dummer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoffrey_Dummer) conceptualised the integrated circuit.[^cite_note-174]&#x20;

The [Royal Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Society), formally [The Royal Society of London for Improving Natural Knowledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Royal_Society_of_London_for_Improving_Natural_Knowledge),[^cite_note-royalsociety.org-175] is a [learned society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Learned_society) and the [United Kingdom](31717.md)'s national [academy of sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_of_sciences). Founded on 28 November 1660,[^cite_note-royalsociety.org-175] it is the oldest national scientific institution in the world.[^cite_note-176] The [Royal Institution of Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Institution) was founded in 1799 by leading English scientists, including [Henry Cavendish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Cavendish).[^cite_note-177] Some experts claim that the earliest concept of a [metric system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metric_system) was invented by [John Wilkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Wilkins) in 1668.[^cite_note-178]&#x20;

Scientific research and development remains important in the [universities of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universities_of_England), with many establishing [science parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_park) to facilitate production and co-operation with industry.[^cite_note-179] [Cambridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon_Fen) is the most intensive research cluster for science and technology in the world.[^cite_note-180] In 2022, the UK produced 6.3 per cent of the world's scientific research papers and had a 10.5 per cent share of scientific citations, the third highest in the world (after the United States and China).[^cite_note-181] Scientific journals produced in England include *[Nature](43427.md)*, the *[British Medical Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BMJ)* and *[The Lancet](463899.md)*. The [Department for Science, Innovation and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_for_Science,_Innovation_and_Technology), [secretary of state for science, innovation and technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Science,_Innovation_and_Technology), and [minister of state for science, research and innovation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_State_for_Science,_Research_and_Innovation) has responsibility for science in England.[^cite_note-182]&#x20;

### Transport

Main article: [Transport in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_England)

The [Department for Transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_for_Transport) is the government body responsible for overseeing transport in England. The department is run by the [secretary of state for transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_state_for_transport).&#x20;

England has a dense and modern transportation infrastructure. There are [many motorways in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_motorways_in_the_United_Kingdom), and many other trunk roads, such as the [A1 Great North Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A1_road_\(Great_Britain\)), which runs through eastern England from London to Newcastle[^cite_note-roads-183] (much of this section is motorway) and onward to the Scottish border. The longest motorway in England is the [M6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M6_motorway), from [Rugby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby,_Warwickshire) through the [North West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_West_England) up to the [Anglo-Scottish border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Scottish_border), a distance of 232 miles (373 km).[^cite_note-roads-183] Other major routes include: the [M1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M1_motorway) from London to Leeds, the [M25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M25_motorway) which encircles London, the [M60](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M60_motorway_\(Great_Britain\)) which encircles Manchester, the [M4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M4_motorway) from London to South Wales, the [M62](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M62_motorway) from Liverpool via Manchester to East Yorkshire, and the [M5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M5_motorway) from Birmingham to Bristol and the South West.[^cite_note-roads-183]&#x20;

red stone building with tall clock tower in corner

[London St Pancras International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Pancras_railway_station) is one of London's main domestic and international transport hubs providing both commuter rail and high-speed rail services across the UK and to Paris, [Lille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lille) and [Brussels](3708.md).

Bus transport across the country is widespread; major companies include [Arriva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arriva_UK_Bus), [FirstGroup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FirstGroup), [Go-Ahead Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Go-Ahead_Group), [Mobico Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobico_Group), [Rotala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotala) and [Stagecoach Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stagecoach_Group). [Bus rapid transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_rapid_transit) originated in England with the [Runcorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Runcorn) Busway opening in 1971.[^cite_note-Runcorn_Busway_JSTOR-184][^cite_note-RUDI_7.3_Transport-185] The red [double-decker buses](902183.md) in London have become a symbol of England. [National Cycle Route](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Cycle_Route) offers cycling routes nationally.&#x20;

[Rail transport in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport_in_Great_Britain) is the oldest in the world: passenger railways originated in England in 1825.[^cite_note-186] Much of Britain's 10,000 miles (16,000 km) of rail network lies in England, covering the country fairly extensively. There is rail access to France and Belgium through the [Channel Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_Tunnel) (completed in 1994) with [Eurostar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurostar) or [LeShuttle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LeShuttle) services.&#x20;

[Great British Railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_British_Railways) is a planned state-owned public body that will oversee [rail transport in Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport_in_Great_Britain) from 2024. The [Office of Rail and Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_Rail_and_Road) is responsible for the economic and safety regulation of England's railways.[^cite_note-187] [Crossrail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crossrail) was Europe's largest construction project with a £15 billion projected cost, opened in 2022.[^cite_note-188] [High Speed 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Speed_2), a high-speed north–south railway line, is under construction since 2019.[^cite_note-189]&#x20;

There is a [rapid transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_transit) network in two English cities: the [London Underground](17839.md), and the [Tyne and Wear Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyne_and_Wear_Metro) in [Newcastle upon Tyne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newcastle_upon_Tyne), [Gateshead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gateshead) and [Sunderland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunderland).[^cite_note-pubwhite-190] There are several extensive tram networks, such as the [Manchester Metrolink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchester_Metrolink), [Sheffield Supertram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheffield_Supertram), [West Midlands Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Midlands_Metro), [Nottingham Express Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nottingham_Express_Transit), and [Tramlink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tramlink) in South London.[^cite_note-pubwhite-190] England also has extensive domestic and international aviation links. The largest airport is [Heathrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heathrow_Airport), which is the [world's second busiest airport measured by number of international passengers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World%27s_busiest_airports_by_international_passenger_traffic).[^cite_note-wsj230211-191]&#x20;

By sea there is ferry transport, both local and international, including from Liverpool to Ireland and the Isle of Man, and Hull to the Netherlands and Belgium.[^cite_note-waterworks-192] There are around 4,400 miles (7,100 km) of navigable waterways in England, half of which is owned by the [Canal & River Trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canal_%26_River_Trust),[^cite_note-waterworks-192] however, water transport is very limited. The [River Thames](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Thames) is the major waterway in England, with imports and exports focused at the [Port of Tilbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Tilbury) in the [Thames Estuary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thames_Estuary), one of the United Kingdom's three major ports.[^cite_note-waterworks-192]&#x20;

### Energy

Main article: [Energy in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_the_United_Kingdom)



Wind turbines at [Den Brook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Den_Brook_Wind_Farm), Devon. The UK is [one of the best sites in Europe for wind energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_power_in_the_United_Kingdom), and wind power production is its fastest growing supply.

[^cite_note-\:1-193]

[^cite_note-Roadmap-194]

Successive governments have outlined numerous commitments to reduce [carbon dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_dioxide) emissions. Notably, the UK is [one of the best sites in Europe for wind energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_power_in_the_United_Kingdom), and wind power production is its fastest growing supply.[^cite_note-Roadmap-194] Wind power contributed 26.8% of UK electricity generation in 2022.[^cite_note-195] England is home to [Hornsea 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hornsea_Wind_Farm), the largest offshore wind farm in the world, situated in waters roughly 89 kilometres off the coast of Yorkshire.[^cite_note-196]&#x20;

The [Climate Change Act 2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_Change_Act_2008) was passed in Parliament with an overwhelming majority across political parties. It sets out emission reduction targets that the UK must comply with legally. It represents the first global legally binding [climate change mitigation](2119179.md) target set by a country.[^cite_note-197] [UK government energy policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_policy_of_the_United_Kingdom) aims to play a key role in limiting [greenhouse gas emissions](9528025.md), while meeting energy demand. Shifting availabilities of resources and development of technologies also change the country's [energy mix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_mix) through changes in costs.[^cite_note-198]&#x20;

The current energy policy is the responsibility of the [Department for Energy Security and Net Zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_for_Energy_Security_and_Net_Zero) and [Secretary of State for Energy Security and Net Zero](72970227.md).[^cite_note-199] The [Minister of State for Business, Energy and Clean Growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_State_for_Business,_Energy_and_Clean_Growth) is responsible for green finance, climate science and innovation, and low carbon generation.[^cite_note-200] In 2024, the United Kingdom was ranked 5 out of 180 countries in the [Environmental Performance Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_Performance_Index).[^cite_note-201] A law has been passed that [UK greenhouse gas emissions](9528025.md) will be [net zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Net_zero) by 2050.[^cite_note-202]&#x20;

## Healthcare

Main article: [Healthcare in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_England)



[William Beveridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Beveridge)'s 1942 report *Social Insurance and Allied Services* served as the basis for the post-[World War II](32927.md) [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state).

The [National Health Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Health_Service_\(England\)) (NHS) is the publicly funded [healthcare system](60976.md) responsible for providing the majority of healthcare in the country. The NHS began on 5 July 1948, putting into effect the provisions of the [National Health Service Act 1946](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Health_Service_Act_1946). It was based on the findings of the [Beveridge Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beveridge_Report), prepared by the economist and social reformer, [William Beveridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Beveridge).[^cite_note-203] The NHS is largely funded by general taxation and [National Insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Insurance) payments;[^cite_note-204] it provides most of its services free at the point of use, although there are charges for some people for eye tests, dental care, prescriptions and aspects of personal care.[^cite_note-205]&#x20;

The government department responsible for the NHS is the [Department of Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Health_\(United_Kingdom\)), under the [secretary of state for health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_state_for_health). Most of the department's expenses are on the NHS – £98.6 billion was spent in 2008–2009.[^cite_note-budget2008-206] Regulatory bodies such as the [General Medical Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Medical_Council) and the [Nursing and Midwifery Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nursing_and_Midwifery_Council) are organised on a UK-wide basis, as are non-governmental bodies such as the [Royal Colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_royal_college).&#x20;

The average [life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_expectancy) is 77.5 years for males and 81.7 years for females, the highest of the four [countries of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Countries_of_the_United_Kingdom).[^cite_note-LifeExpect-207] The south of England has a higher life expectancy than the north, but regional differences seem to be slowly narrowing: between 1991–1993 and 2012–2014, life expectancy in the North East increased by 6.0 years and in the North West by 5.8 years.[^cite_note-LifeExpect-207]&#x20;

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_England)

### Population

Main article: [English people](18803164.md)

See also: [English diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_diaspora), [Cornish people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornish_people), and [List of urban areas in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_urban_areas_in_the_United_Kingdom)

Map of England with regions shaded in different shades of blue.

The [metropolitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_county) and [non-metropolitan counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-metropolitan_county), colour-coded to show population



Population of England and Wales by administrative areas. Their size shows their population, with some approximation. Each group of squares in the map key is 20% of total number of districts.

With over 56 million inhabitants, England is the most populous country of the United Kingdom, accounting for 84% of the combined total.[^cite_note-ONS_mid-year_pop_est-11] England taken as a unit and measured against international states would be the 26th largest [country by population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population) in the world.[^cite_note-208]&#x20;

The [English people](18803164.md) are [British people](19097669.md).[^cite_note-ethnicityengl-209] There is an English diaspora in former parts of the British Empire; especially the [United States](3434750.md), [Canada](5042916.md), [Australia](4689264.md), [South Africa](17416221.md) and [New Zealand](4913064.md).[^cite_note-214] Since the late 1990s, many English people [have migrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_migration_to_Spain) to Spain.[^cite_note-215] Due in particular to the economic prosperity of [South East England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_East_England), it has received many economic migrants from the other parts of the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-ethnicityengl-209] There has been [significant Irish migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_migration_to_Great_Britain).[^cite_note-216] The proportion of ethnically European residents totals at 81.7%,[^cite_note-ethnicityengl-2021-217] including [White British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_British), [Germans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Germans)[^cite_note-migra-218] and [Poles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_British),[^cite_note-ethnicityengl-209] down from 94.1% in 1991.[^cite_note-ethnicityengl-209] Other people from much further afield in the former British colonies have arrived since the 1950s: in particular, about 7% of people living in England have family [origins in the Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asians_in_the_United_Kingdom), mostly [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Indians), [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Pakistanis) and [Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Bangladeshis).[^cite_note-ethnicityengl-2021-217][^cite_note-migra-218] About 0.7% are [Chinese](1255834.md),[^cite_note-ethnicityengl-2021-217][^cite_note-migra-218] 0.6% are [Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Arabs).[^cite_note-ethnicityengl-2021-217] 4.0% of the population are [black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_British_people), from Africa and the [Caribbean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_African-Caribbean_people), especially former British colonies,[^cite_note-ethnicityengl-2021-217][^cite_note-migra-218] and 2.9% identified as multiracial or [mixed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_\(United_Kingdom_ethnicity_category\)).[^cite_note-ethnicityengl-2021-217]&#x20;

In 2007, 22% of primary school children in England were from [ethnic minority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_group) families,[^cite_note-Paton-219] and in 2011 that figure was 26.5%.[^cite_note-Shepherd-220] About half of the population increase between 1991 and 2001 was due to immigration.[^cite_note-221]&#x20;

England contains one indigenous national minority, the [Cornish people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornish_people), recognised by the UK government under the [Framework Convention for the Protection of National Minorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Framework_Convention_for_the_Protection_of_National_Minorities) in 2014.[^cite_note-ReferenceB-222]&#x20;

### Language

Further information: [Languages of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_the_United_Kingdom) and [English language in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language_in_England)

| Language | Native speakers (thousands)[^cite_note-223] |
| --- | --- |
| [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language_in_England) | 46,937 |
| [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_language) | 529 |
| [Punjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_language) | 272 |
| [Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu) | 266 |
| [Bengali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_language) | 216 |
| [Gujarati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gujarati_language) | 212 |
| [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) | 152 |
| [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language) | 145 |
| [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language) | 131 |
| [Welsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_language) | 8 |
| [Cornish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornish_language) | 0.6 |
| Other | 2,267 |
| Population | 51,006 |

[English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language), today widely spoken around the world,[^cite_note-224] originated in what is now England, where it remains the principal tongue. According to a 2011 census, it is spoken well or very well by 98% of the population[^cite_note-CensusEnglish-225]&#x20;

[English language learning and teaching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_as_a_second_or_foreign_language) is an important [economic activity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economics). There is no [legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_legislation) mandating an [official language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_language) for England,[^cite_note-226] but English is the only language used for official business. Despite the country's relatively small size, there are many distinct [regional accents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_accents_of_English_speakers#England).&#x20;

Cornish died out as a community language in the 18th century but is being revived,[^cite_note-227] and is now protected under the [European Charter for Regional or Minority Languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Charter_for_Regional_or_Minority_Languages).[^cite_note-228] It is spoken by 0.1% of people in [Cornwall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornwall),[^cite_note-229] and is taught to some degree in several primary and secondary schools.[^cite_note-230]&#x20;

[State schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_school) teach students a [second language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_language) or [third language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_language) from the ages of seven, most commonly French, Spanish or German.[^cite_note-231] It was reported in 2007 that around 800,000 school students spoke a foreign language at home,[^cite_note-Paton-219] the most common being [Punjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_language) and [Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu). However, following the 2011 census data released by the [Office for National Statistics](324029.md), figures now show that Polish is the main language spoken in England after English.[^cite_note-232] In 2022, [British Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Sign_Language) became an official language of England when the [British Sign Language Act 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Sign_Language_Act_2022) came into effect.[^cite_note-233]&#x20;

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_England)

Further information: [History of Christianity in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Christianity_in_England)

In the 2021 census, 46.3% of the population of England specified their religion as [Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian), 36.7% answered that they had no religion, 6.7% specified that they were [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim), while 4.3% of the population belongs to other religions and 6% did not give an answer.[^cite_note-234] Christianity is the most widely practised religion in England. The [established church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Established_church) of England is the [Church of England](5955.md),[^cite_note-235] which left communion with [Rome](32408.md) in the 1530s when [Henry VIII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_VIII_of_England) was unable to annul his marriage to [Catherine of Aragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_of_Aragon). The church regards itself as both Catholic and [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant).[^cite_note-236]&#x20;

There are [High Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Church) and [Low Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_Church) traditions, and some Anglicans regard themselves as [Anglo-Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Catholics), following the [Tractarian movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tractarian_movement). The monarch of the United Kingdom is the [supreme governor of the Church of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_governor_of_the_Church_of_England), which has around 26 million baptised members (of whom the vast majority are not regular churchgoers). In 2016, research at [St. Mary's University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Mary%27s_University,_Twickenham) in London reported that 19.8% of the population of England and Wales identified as Anglican.[^cite_note-\:3-237][^cite_note-\:4-238] The Church of England forms part of the [Anglican Communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican_Communion), with the [Archbishop of Canterbury](2345.md) acting as its symbolic worldwide head.[^cite_note-239] Many [cathedrals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cathedrals_in_England) and parish churches are historic buildings of significant architectural importance, such as [Westminster Abbey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westminster_Abbey), [York Minster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/York_Minster), [Durham Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durham_Cathedral), and [Salisbury Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salisbury_Cathedral).&#x20;



[Westminster Abbey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westminster_Abbey) is a notable example of [English Gothic architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Gothic_architecture). The [coronation of the British monarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronation_of_the_British_monarch) traditionally takes place at the Abbey.

The second-largest Christian denomination is the [Catholic Church](606848.md). Since its reintroduction after the [Catholic Emancipation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Emancipation), the Church has organised ecclesiastically on an [England and Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church_in_England_and_Wales) basis where there are 4.5 million members (most of whom are English).[^cite_note-240] In 2016, 8.3% of the population of England and [Wales](69894.md) identified as Catholic.[^cite_note-\:3-237][^cite_note-\:4-238] There has been one Pope from England to date, [Adrian IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Adrian_IV), while saints [Bede](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bede), [Anselm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anselm_of_Canterbury), and [John Henry Newman](83895.md) are regarded as [Doctors of the Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctors_of_the_Church).&#x20;

A form of [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) known as [Methodism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodist_Church_of_Great_Britain) is the third largest Christian practice; it grew out of Anglicanism through [John Wesley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Wesley).[^cite_note-241] It gained popularity in the [mill towns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mill_town) of [Lancashire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancashire) and [Yorkshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yorkshire), and amongst tin miners in [Cornwall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornwall).[^cite_note-242] There are other [nonconformist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonconformist_\(Protestantism\)) minorities, such as [Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptists), [Quakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quakers), [Congregationalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregational_church), [Unitarians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitarianism) and [The Salvation Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Salvation_Army).[^cite_note-243]&#x20;

The patron saint of England is [Saint George](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_George); his symbolic cross is included in the flag of England.[^cite_note-flaghistory-244] There are many other English and associated saints, including [Cuthbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuthbert), [Edmund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_the_Martyr), [Alban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Alban), [Wilfrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilfrid), [Aidan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aidan_of_Lindisfarne), [Edward the Confessor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_the_Confessor), [John Fisher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Fisher), [Thomas More](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_More), [Petroc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Petroc), [Piran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Piran), [Margaret Clitherow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Clitherow) and [Thomas Becket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Becket). There are non-Christian religions practised. [Jews](25955086.md) have a history of a small minority on the island since 1070.[^cite_note-jews-245] They were expelled from England in 1290 following the [Edict of Expulsion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Expulsion), and were allowed back in 1656.[^cite_note-jews-245]&#x20;

Especially since the 1950s, religions from the [former British colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) have grown in numbers, due to immigration. [Islam](6037917.md) is the most common of these, now accounting for around 5% of the population in England.[^cite_note-statsio-246] [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism), [Sikhism](27964.md) and [Buddhism](3267529.md) are next in number, adding up to 2.8% combined,[^cite_note-statsio-246] introduced from India and [Southeast Asia](28741.md).[^cite_note-statsio-246]&#x20;

A small minority of the population practise ancient [Pagan religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pagan_religion). [Neopaganism in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neopaganism_in_the_United_Kingdom) is primarily represented by [Wicca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wicca) and [Neopagan witchcraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neopagan_witchcraft), [Druidry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Druidry), and [Heathenry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic_neopaganism). According to the [2011 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_Census_2011), there are roughly 53,172 people who identify as Pagan in England,[^cite_note-pagan-248] including 11,026 [Wiccans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wicca).[^cite_note-249] 24.7% of people in England declared [no religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion), compared with 14.6% in 2001.[^cite_note-2011_ONS_results-247] [Norwich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwich) had the highest such proportion at 42.5%, followed by [Brighton and Hove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brighton_and_Hove) at 42.4%.&#x20;

## Education

Main article: [Education in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_England)

The [Department for Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_for_Education) is the government department responsible for issues affecting people in England up to the age of 19, including education.[^cite_note-250] State-funded schools are attended by about 93% of English schoolchildren.[^cite_note-251] Education is the responsibility of the [secretary of state for education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_state_for_education).[^cite_note-252]&#x20;

&#x20;Children between the ages of 3 and 5 attend nursery or an [Early Years Foundation Stage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Years_Foundation_Stage) reception unit within a primary school. Children between the ages of 5 and 11 attend primary school, and secondary school is attended by those aged between 11 and 16. [State-funded schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State-funded_schools_\(England\)) are obliged by law to teach the [National Curriculum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Curriculum_for_England); basic areas of learning include [English literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_literature), English language, [mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematics_education), science, art & design, citizenship, history, geography, religious education, design & technology, computing, ancient & modern languages, music, and physical education.[^cite_note-253]



The [University of Oxford](31797.md) was founded in 1096, making it the world's [second-oldest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_oldest_universities_in_continuous_operation) university.

&#x20;The [Programme for International Student Assessment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Programme_for_International_Student_Assessment) coordinated by the [OECD](33853117.md) currently ranks the overall knowledge and skills of British 15-year-olds as 13th in the world in literacy, mathematics, and science with the average British student scoring 503.7, well above the OECD average of 493.[^cite_note-254]&#x20;

Although most English secondary schools are comprehensive, there are selective intake [grammar schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammar_school) to which entrance is subject to passing the [eleven-plus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eleven-plus) exam. Around 7.2 per cent of English schoolchildren attend [private schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_school_\(UK\)), which are funded by private sources.[^cite_note-255] Standards in state schools are monitored by the [Office for Standards in Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_for_Standards_in_Education), and in private schools by the [Independent Schools Inspectorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_Schools_Inspectorate).[^cite_note-256]&#x20;

After finishing compulsory education, students take [GCSE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Certificate_of_Secondary_Education) examinations. Students may then opt to continue into [further education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Further_education) for two years. [Further education colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_further_education_colleges_in_England) (particularly [sixth form colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sixth_form_colleges)) often form part of a secondary school site. [A-level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A-level) examinations are sat by a large number of further education students, and often form the basis of an application to university. Further education covers a wide curriculum of study and [apprenticeships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apprenticeship), including [T-levels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T_Level), [BTEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_and_Technology_Education_Council), [NVQ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NVQ) and others. [Tertiary colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_college) provide both academic and vocational courses.[^cite_note-257]&#x20;

### Higher education

Higher education students normally attend university from age 18 onwards, where they study for an [academic degree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_degree). There are over 90 universities in England, all but one of which are [public institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_university). The [Department for Business, Innovation and Skills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_for_Business,_Innovation_and_Skills) is the government department responsible for higher education in England.[^cite_note-258] Students are generally entitled to [student loans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_loan) to cover [tuition fees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuition_fees) and living costs.[^cite_note-260] The first degree offered to undergraduates is the [bachelor's degree](188874.md), which usually takes three years to complete. Students are then able to work towards a postgraduate degree, which usually takes one year, or a doctorate, which takes three or more years.[^cite_note-FHEQ-261]&#x20;

[England's universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_in_England) include some of the highest-ranked universities in the world. As of 2024, four England-based universities, the [University of Cambridge](25978572.md), [University of Oxford](31797.md), [Imperial College London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_College_London), and [University College London](52029.md), are ranked amongst the top ten in the 2024 *[QS World University Rankings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QS_World_University_Rankings)*. The University of Cambridge, founded in 1209, and the University of Oxford, founded in 1096, are the two [oldest universities in the English-speaking world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_oldest_universities_in_continuous_operation).[^cite_note-262]&#x20;

The [London School of Economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_School_of_Economics) has been described as the world's leading social science institution for both teaching and research.[^cite_note-london_156-263] The [London Business School](520187.md) is considered one of the world's leading business schools and in 2010 its MBA programme was ranked best in the world by the *[Financial Times](136566.md)*.[^cite_note-ft-264] [Academic degrees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_degree) in England are usually split into classes: first class, upper second class, lower second class, third, and unclassified.[^cite_note-FHEQ-261] [The King's School, Canterbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_King%27s_School,_Canterbury) and [King's School, Rochester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King%27s_School,_Rochester) are the oldest schools in the English-speaking world.[^cite_note-265] Many of England's most well-known schools, such as [Winchester College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winchester_College), [Eton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eton_College), [St Paul's School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Paul%27s_School_\(London\)), [Harrow School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harrow_School) and [Rugby School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_School) are fee-paying institutions.[^cite_note-266]&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_England)

Further information: [English Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Renaissance)

### Architecture

Main article: [Architecture of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_England)

Many ancient [standing stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standing_stone) monuments were erected during the prehistoric period; amongst the best known are [Stonehenge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonehenge), [Devil's Arrows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devil%27s_Arrows), [Rudston Monolith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudston_Monolith) and [Castlerigg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castlerigg_stone_circle).[^cite_note-267] With the introduction of [Ancient Roman architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Roman_architecture) there was a development of [basilicas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilicas), [baths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_baths), [amphitheaters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amphitheaters), [triumphal arches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triumphal_arch), [villas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Villa), [Roman temples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_temple), [Roman roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_road), [Roman forts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_fort), [stockades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stockade) and [aqueducts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqueduct_\(bridge\)).[^cite_note-roman-268] It was the Romans who founded the first cities and towns such as London, Bath, York, Chester and St Albans. Perhaps the best-known example is [Hadrian's Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadrian%27s_Wall) stretching right across northern England.[^cite_note-roman-268] Another well-preserved example is the [Roman Baths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Baths_\(Bath\)) at [Bath, Somerset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bath,_Somerset).[^cite_note-roman-268]&#x20;

A castle of square plan surrounded by a water-filled moat. It has round corner towers and a forbidding appearance.

[Bodiam Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodiam_Castle) is a 14th-century [moated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moat) [castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castle) near [Robertsbridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robertsbridge) in East Sussex.

[Early medieval architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Saxon_architecture)'s secular buildings were simple constructions mainly using timber with [thatch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thatch) for roofing. Ecclesiastical architecture ranged from a synthesis of [Hiberno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Christian_Ireland)–[Saxon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic_Christianity) [monasticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monasticism),[^cite_note-269][^cite_note-270] to [Early Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Christian_art_and_architecture) [basilica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica) and architecture characterised by pilaster-strips, blank arcading, baluster shafts and triangular headed openings. After the Norman conquest in 1066 various [castles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castles_in_England) were created; the best known include the [Tower of London](31165.md), [Warwick Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warwick_Castle), [Durham Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durham_Castle) and [Windsor Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Windsor_Castle).[^cite_note-buildings-271]&#x20;

Throughout the Plantagenet era, an [English Gothic architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Gothic_architecture) flourished, with prime examples including the [medieval cathedrals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_the_medieval_cathedrals_of_England) such as [Canterbury Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canterbury_Cathedral), [Westminster Abbey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westminster_Abbey) and [York Minster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/York_Minster).[^cite_note-buildings-271] Expanding on the [Norman base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_architecture) there was also [castles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castle), [palaces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace), [great houses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_historic_houses_in_England), universities and [parish churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parish_church). Medieval architecture was completed with the 16th-century [Tudor style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tudor_architecture); the four-centred arch, now known as the [Tudor arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tudor_arch), was a defining feature as were [wattle and daub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wattle_and_daub) houses domestically. In the aftermath of the [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance) a form of architecture echoing classical antiquity synthesised with Christianity appeared, the [English Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Baroque) style of architect [Christopher Wren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Wren) being particularly championed.[^cite_note-272]&#x20;

[Georgian architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_architecture) followed in a more refined style, evoking a simple Palladian form; the [Royal Crescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Crescent) at Bath is one of the best examples of this. With the emergence of [romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism) during Victorian period, a [Gothic Revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_Revival) was launched. In addition to this, around the same time the Industrial Revolution paved the way for buildings such as [The Crystal Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Crystal_Palace). Since the 1930s various [modernist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernist_architecture) forms have appeared whose reception is often controversial, though traditionalist resistance movements continue with support in influential places.[^cite_note-274]&#x20;

### Gardens

Main article: [English garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_garden)



The landscape garden at Stourhead. Inspired by the great [landscape artists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landscape_artists) of the seventeenth century, the landscape garden was described as a "living work of art" when first opened in the 1750s.

[^cite_note-275]

Landscape gardening, as developed by [Capability Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capability_Brown), set an international trend for the [English landscape garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_landscape_garden). Gardening, and visiting gardens, are regarded as typically English pursuits. The English garden presented an idealised view of nature. At large country houses, the English garden usually included lakes, sweeps of gently rolling lawns set against groves of trees, and recreations of classical temples, [Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_architecture) ruins, bridges, and other picturesque architecture, designed to recreate an idyllic pastoral landscape.[^cite_note-\:2-276]&#x20;

By the end of the 18th century, the English garden was being imitated by the [French landscape garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_landscape_garden), and as far away as [Pavlovsk, Saint Petersburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavlovsk,_Saint_Petersburg), the gardens of the future [Emperor Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emperor_Paul). It also had a major influence on the [public parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_parks) and gardens which appeared around the world in the 19th century.[^cite_note-277] The English landscape garden was centred on the [English country house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_country_house) and manor houses.[^cite_note-\:2-276]&#x20;

[English Heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Heritage) and the [National Trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Trust) preserve great gardens and landscape parks throughout the country.[^cite_note-278] The [RHS Chelsea Flower Show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RHS_Chelsea_Flower_Show) is held every year by the [Royal Horticultural Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Horticultural_Society) and is said to be the largest gardening show in the world.[^cite_note-279]&#x20;

### Folklore

Main article: [English folklore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_folklore)



[Robin Hood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robin_Hood) and [Maid Marian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maid_Marian) with [Richard I of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_I_of_England)

English folklore developed over many centuries. Some of the characters and stories are present across England, but most belong to specific regions. Common folkloric beings include [pixies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pixie), [giants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giant), [elves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elf), [bogeymen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bogeyman), [trolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troll), [goblins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goblin), and [dwarves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwarf_\(folklore\)). While many legends and folk-customs are thought to be ancient, such as the tales featuring [Offa of Angel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Offa_of_Angel) and [Wayland the Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayland_the_Smith),[^cite_note-keary-280] others date from after the Norman invasion. The legends featuring [Robin Hood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robin_Hood) and his [Merry Men](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merry_Men) of [Sherwood](28739.md), and their battles with the [Sheriff of Nottingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheriff_of_Nottingham), are amongst the best-known of these.[^cite_note-281]&#x20;

During the [High Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Middle_Ages) tales originating from Brythonic traditions entered English folklore and developed into the [Arthurian myth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthurian_myth).[^cite_note-woodbbc-282][^cite_note-higham1-283][^cite_note-284] These were derived from [Anglo-Norman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Norman_language), Welsh and French sources,[^cite_note-higham1-283] featuring [King Arthur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Arthur), [Camelot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camelot), [Excalibur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excalibur), [Merlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merlin) and the [Knights of the Round Table](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knights_of_the_Round_Table) such as [Lancelot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancelot). These stories are most centrally brought together within [Geoffrey of Monmouth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoffrey_of_Monmouth)'s *[Historia Regum Britanniae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historia_Regum_Britanniae)* (*History of the Kings of Britain*).[^cite_note-286]&#x20;

Some folk figures are based on semi or actual historical people whose story has been passed down centuries.[^cite_note-287] On 5 November people celebrate [Bonfire Night](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonfire_Night) to commemorate the foiling of the [Gunpowder Plot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gunpowder_Plot) centred on [Guy Fawkes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guy_Fawkes). There are various national and regional folk activities, participated in to this day, such as [Morris dancing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morris_dancing), [Maypole dancing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maypole_dance), [Rapper sword](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapper_sword) in the North East, [Long Sword dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_Sword_dance) in Yorkshire, [Mummers Plays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mummers_Play), [bottle-kicking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bottle-kicking) in Leicestershire, and [cheese-rolling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooper%27s_Hill_Cheese-Rolling_and_Wake) at [Cooper's Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brockworth,_Gloucestershire).[^cite_note-288] There is no official national costume, but a few are well established such as the [Pearly Kings and Queens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pearly_Kings_and_Queens) associated with cockneys, the [Royal Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen%27s_Guard), the [Morris costume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morris_dance) and [Beefeaters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yeomen_Warders).[^cite_note-289]&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [English cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_cuisine)

&#x20;Since the [early modern period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_modern_period) the food of England has historically been characterised by its simplicity of approach and a reliance on the high quality of natural produce.[^cite_note-290] During the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages) and the Renaissance, English cuisine enjoyed an excellent reputation, though a decline began during the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution) with increasing urbanisation. The cuisine of England has, however, recently undergone a revival, which has been recognised by food critics with some good ratings in *[Restaurant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restaurant_\(magazine\))*'s [best restaurant in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Best_restaurant_in_the_world) charts.[^cite_note-291]



English foods: clockwise from top left—[teacakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teacakes), [cheeses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_cheese), [wines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_wine) and [cider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cider)

Traditional examples of English food include the [Sunday roast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunday_roast), featuring a [roasted joint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roasting) (usually beef, [lamb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lamb_and_mutton), chicken or pork) served with assorted vegetables, [Yorkshire pudding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yorkshire_pudding) and [gravy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravy).[^cite_note-tradfood-292] Other prominent meals include [fish and chips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish_and_chips) and the [full English breakfast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_breakfast) (generally consisting of [bacon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bacon), [sausages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sausages), grilled tomatoes, fried bread, [black pudding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_pudding), [baked beans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baked_beans), [mushrooms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edible_mushroom) and eggs).[^cite_note-293] Various [meat pies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meat_pie) are consumed, such as [steak and kidney pie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steak_and_kidney_pie), [steak and ale pie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steak_and_ale_pie), [cottage pie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cottage_pie), [pork pie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pork_pie) (usually eaten cold)[^cite_note-tradfood-292] and the [Cornish pasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornish_pasty).&#x20;

Sausages are commonly eaten, either as [bangers and mash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangers_and_mash) or as [toad in the hole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toad_in_the_hole). [Lancashire hotpot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancashire_hotpot) is a well-known stew originating in the northwest. Some of the more popular cheeses are [Cheddar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheddar_cheese), [Red Leicester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Leicester), [Wensleydale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wensleydale_\(cheese\)), [Double Gloucester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_Gloucester) and [Blue Stilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stilton_cheese). Many [Anglo-Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Indian) hybrid dishes, [curries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curries), have been created, such as [chicken tikka masala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicken_tikka_masala) and [balti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balti_\(food\)). Traditional English dessert dishes include [apple pie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_pie) and other fruit pies; [spotted dick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spotted_dick) – all generally served with [custard](62149.md); and, more recently, [sticky toffee pudding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sticky_toffee_pudding). Sweet pastries include [scones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scones) served with jam or cream, dried fruit loaves, [Eccles cakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eccles_cake) and [mince pies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mince_pie) as well as sweet or spiced biscuits.&#x20;

Common non-alcoholic drinks include tea[^cite_note-294] and coffee; frequently consumed alcoholic drinks include wine, [ciders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cider) and [English beers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_beer), such as [bitter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitter_\(beer\)), [mild](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mild_ale), [stout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stout) and [brown ale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_ale).[^cite_note-295]&#x20;

### Visual arts

Main article: [English art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_art)

See also: [Arts Council England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arts_Council_England)

A horse-drawn wagon crossing a river towards a cottage, with trees and fields beyond

*[The Hay Wain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hay_Wain)* by [John Constable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Constable), 1821, is an archetypal English painting.

A painting of a red haired woman, sitting in a boat, surrounded by trees

*[The Lady of Shalott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Lady_of_Shalott_\(painting\))* by [John William Waterhouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_William_Waterhouse), 1888, in the [Pre-Raphaelite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Raphaelite) style

The earliest known examples are the prehistoric rock and [cave art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cave_art) pieces, most prominent in [North Yorkshire](143759.md), Northumberland and [Cumbria](73021.md), but also feature further south, for example at [Creswell Crags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creswell_Crags).[^cite_note-296] With the arrival of [Roman culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_culture) in the 1st century, various forms of art such as statues, busts, glasswork and mosaics were the norm. There are numerous surviving artefacts, such as those at [Lullingstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lullingstone_Roman_Villa) and [Aldborough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isurium_Brigantum).[^cite_note-297] During the Early Middle Ages the style favoured sculpted crosses and ivories, manuscript painting, gold and enamel jewellery, demonstrating a love of intricate, interwoven designs such as in the [Staffordshire Hoard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Staffordshire_Hoard) discovered in 2009. Some of these blended [Gaelic and Anglian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insular_art) styles, such as the [Lindisfarne Gospels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lindisfarne_Gospels) and [Vespasian Psalter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vespasian_Psalter).[^cite_note-298] Later [Gothic art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_art) was popular at Winchester and Canterbury, examples survive such as [Benedictional of St. Æthelwold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benedictional_of_St._%C3%86thelwold) and [Luttrell Psalter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luttrell_Psalter).[^cite_note-engart-299]&#x20;

The Tudor era saw [prominent artists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artists_of_the_Tudor_court) as part of their court; portrait painting, which would remain an enduring part of English art, was boosted by German [Hans Holbein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_Holbein_the_Younger), and natives such as [Nicholas Hilliard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_Hilliard) built on this.[^cite_note-engart-299] Under the Stuarts, Continental artists were influential especially the Flemish, examples from the period include [Anthony van Dyck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_van_Dyck), [Peter Lely](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Lely), [Godfrey Kneller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Godfrey_Kneller) and [William Dobson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Dobson).[^cite_note-engart-299] The 18th century saw the founding of the [Royal Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Academy); a [classicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classicism) based on the [High Renaissance](311401.md) prevailed, with [Thomas Gainsborough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Gainsborough) and [Joshua Reynolds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joshua_Reynolds) becoming two of England's most treasured artists.[^cite_note-engart-299]&#x20;

In the 19th century, [John Constable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Constable) and [J. M. W. Turner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._M._W._Turner) were major landscape artists. The [Norwich School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwich_School_\(art_movement\)) continued the landscape tradition, while the [Pre-Raphaelite Brotherhood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Raphaelite_Brotherhood), led by artists such as [Holman Hunt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holman_Hunt), [Dante Gabriel Rossetti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dante_Gabriel_Rossetti) and [John Everett Millais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Everett_Millais), revived the [Early Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Renaissance) style with their vivid and detailed style.[^cite_note-engart-299] Prominent amongst 20th-century artists was [Henry Moore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Moore), regarded as the voice of British sculpture, and of British modernism in general.[^cite_note-300] The [Royal Society of Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Society_of_Arts) is an organisation committed to the arts.[^cite_note-301]&#x20;

### Literature, drama and philosophy

Main articles: [English literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_literature) and [English drama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_drama)

See also: [Theatre of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatre_of_the_United_Kingdom)

A man dressed in grey with a beard, holding a rosary, depicted next to a coat of arms.

[Geoffrey Chaucer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoffrey_Chaucer) was an English author, poet and philosopher, best remembered for his unfinished frame narrative *[The Canterbury Tales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Canterbury_Tales)*.

Early authors such as [Bede](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bede) and [Alcuin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcuin) wrote in Latin.[^cite_note-warnancmod-302] The period of [Old English literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_English_literature) provided the epic poem *[Beowulf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beowulf)* and the secular prose of the *[Anglo-Saxon Chronicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Saxon_Chronicle)*,[^cite_note-303] along with Christian writings such as *[Judith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judith_\(poem\))*, [Cædmon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%A6dmon)'s *[Hymn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%A6dmon)* and [hagiographies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagiography).[^cite_note-warnancmod-302] Following the Norman conquest [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_literature) continued amongst the educated classes, as well as an [Anglo-Norman literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Norman_literature).&#x20;

[Middle English literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_English_literature) emerged with [Geoffrey Chaucer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoffrey_Chaucer), author of *[The Canterbury Tales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Canterbury_Tales)*, along with [Gower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Gower), the [Pearl Poet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pearl_Poet) and [Langland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Langland). [William of Ockham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_of_Ockham) and [Roger Bacon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Bacon), who were [Franciscans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franciscans), were major philosophers of the Middle Ages. [Julian of Norwich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julian_of_Norwich), who wrote *[Revelations of Divine Love](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revelations_of_Divine_Love)*, was a prominent Christian mystic. With the [English Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Renaissance) literature in the [Early Modern English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Modern_English) style appeared. [William Shakespeare](32897.md), whose works include *[Hamlet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamlet)*, *[Romeo and Juliet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romeo_and_Juliet)*, *[Macbeth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macbeth)*, and *[A Midsummer Night's Dream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Midsummer_Night%27s_Dream)*, remains one of the most championed authors in [English literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_literature).[^cite_note-304]&#x20;

[Christopher Marlowe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Marlowe), [Edmund Spenser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Spenser), [Philip Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_Sydney), [Thomas Kyd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Kyd), [John Donne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Donne), and [Ben Jonson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Jonson) are other established authors of the [Elizabethan age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabethan_literature).[^cite_note-elizren-305] [Francis Bacon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Bacon) and [Thomas Hobbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Hobbes) wrote on [empiricism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empiricism) and [materialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Materialism), including [scientific method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method) and [social contract](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_contract).[^cite_note-elizren-305] [Filmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Filmer) wrote on the [Divine Right of Kings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_Right_of_Kings). [Marvell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Marvell) was the best-known poet of the [Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_England),[^cite_note-306] while [John Milton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Milton) authored *[Paradise Lost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paradise_Lost)* during the [Restoration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restoration_literature).&#x20;

> This royal throne of kings, this sceptred isle, this earth of majesty, this seat of Mars, this other Eden, demi-paradise; this fortress, built by nature for herself. This blessed plot, this earth, this realm, this England.&#x20;

— [William Shakespeare](32897.md).

[^cite_note-307]

Some of the most prominent philosophers of the [Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment) were [John Locke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Locke), [Thomas Paine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Paine), [Samuel Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Johnson) and [Jeremy Bentham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeremy_Bentham). More radical elements were later countered by [Edmund Burke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Burke) who is regarded as the founder of conservatism.[^cite_note-308] The poet [Alexander Pope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Pope) with his satirical verse became well regarded. The English played a significant role in [romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism): [Samuel Taylor Coleridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Taylor_Coleridge), [Lord Byron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Byron), [John Keats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Keats), [Mary Shelley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Shelley), [Percy Bysshe Shelley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Percy_Bysshe_Shelley), [William Blake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Blake) and [William Wordsworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Wordsworth) were major figures.[^cite_note-309]&#x20;

In response to the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution), agrarian writers sought a way between [liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty) and tradition; [William Cobbett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Cobbett), [G. K. Chesterton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G._K._Chesterton) and [Hilaire Belloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hilaire_Belloc) were main exponents, while the founder of [guild socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guild_socialism), [Arthur Penty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Penty), and [cooperative movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooperative_movement) advocate [G. D. H. Cole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G._D._H._Cole) are somewhat related.[^cite_note-310] Empiricism continued through [John Stuart Mill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Stuart_Mill) and [Bertrand Russell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bertrand_Russell), while [Bernard Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Williams) was involved in [analytics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Analytics). Authors from around the [Victorian era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victorian_era) include [Charles Dickens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Dickens), the [Brontë sisters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bront%C3%AB_sisters), [Jane Austen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jane_Austen), [George Eliot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Eliot), [Rudyard Kipling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudyard_Kipling), [Thomas Hardy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Hardy), [H. G. Wells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H._G._Wells) and [Lewis Carroll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis_Carroll).[^cite_note-311] Since then England has continued to produce novelists such as [George Orwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Orwell), [D. H. Lawrence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D._H._Lawrence), [Virginia Woolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Woolf), [C. S. Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._S._Lewis), [Enid Blyton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enid_Blyton), [Aldous Huxley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aldous_Huxley), [Agatha Christie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agatha_Christie), [Terry Pratchett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terry_Pratchett), [J. R. R. Tolkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._R._R._Tolkien), and [J. K. Rowling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._K._Rowling).[^cite_note-312]&#x20;

### Performing arts

Further information: [Folk music of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_music_of_England)

See also: [Music of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_the_United_Kingdom)

The traditional [folk music of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_folk_music) is centuries old and has contributed to several genres prominently; mostly [sea shanties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_shanties), [jigs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jig), [hornpipes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hornpipe) and [dance music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dance_music). It has its own distinct variations and regional peculiarities. Ballads featuring Robin Hood, printed by [Wynkyn de Worde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wynkyn_de_Worde) in the 16th century, are an important artefact, as are [John Playford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Playford)'s *[The Dancing Master](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Dancing_Master)* and [Robert Harley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Harley,_1st_Earl_of_Oxford_and_Earl_Mortimer)'s *[Roxburghe Ballads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roxburghe_Ballads)* collections.[^cite_note-313] Some of the best-known songs are *[Greensleeves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greensleeves)*, *[Pastime with Good Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pastime_with_Good_Company)*, *[Maggie May](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maggie_May_\(folk_song\))* and *[Spanish Ladies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Ladies)* amongst others. Many [nursery rhymes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nursery_rhymes) are of English origin such as *[Mary, Mary, Quite Contrary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary,_Mary,_Quite_Contrary)*, *[Roses Are Red](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roses_Are_Red)*, *[Jack and Jill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_and_Jill_\(nursery_rhyme\))*, *[London Bridge Is Falling Down](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Bridge_Is_Falling_Down), [The Grand Old Duke of York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Grand_Old_Duke_of_York), [Hey Diddle Diddle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hey_Diddle_Diddle)* and *[Humpty Dumpty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humpty_Dumpty)*.[^cite_note-314] Traditional English Christmas carols include "[We Wish You a Merry Christmas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/We_Wish_You_a_Merry_Christmas)", "[The First Noel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_First_Noel)", "[I Saw Three Ships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I_Saw_Three_Ships)" and "[God Rest Ye Merry, Gentlemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_Rest_Ye_Merry,_Gentlemen)".&#x20;

Early English composers in classical music include Renaissance artists [Thomas Tallis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Tallis) and [William Byrd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Byrd), followed by [Henry Purcell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Purcell) from the [Baroque period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque_music) and [Thomas Arne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Arne) who was well known for his patriotic song "[Rule, Britannia!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rule,_Britannia!)". German-born [George Frideric Handel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Frideric_Handel) spent most of his composing life in London and became a national icon in Britain, creating some of the most well-known works of classical music, especially his English oratorios, *[The Messiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiah_\(Handel\))*, *[Solomon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon_\(Handel\))*, *[Water Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_Music_\(Handel\))*, and *[Music for the Royal Fireworks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_for_the_Royal_Fireworks)*.[^cite_note-315]&#x20;



[The Beatles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Beatles) are the most commercially successful and critically acclaimed band in popular music.

[^cite_note-316]

Classical music attracted much attention in the 18th century with the formation of the [Birmingham Triennial Music Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham_Triennial_Music_Festival), which was the longest running classical music festival of its kind until the final concerts in 1912. The [English Musical Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Musical_Renaissance) was a hypothetical development in the late 19th and early 20th century, when English composers, often those lecturing or trained at the [Royal College of Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_College_of_Music), were said to have freed themselves from foreign musical influences. There was a revival in the profile of composers from England in the 20th century led by [Edward Elgar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Elgar), [Benjamin Britten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Britten), [Frederick Delius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Delius), [Gustav Holst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustav_Holst), [Ralph Vaughan Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ralph_Vaughan_Williams) and others.[^cite_note-317] Present-day composers from England include [Michael Nyman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Nyman), best known for *[The Piano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Piano)*, and [Andrew Lloyd Webber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Lloyd_Webber), whose musicals have achieved enormous success in the [West End](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_End_theatre) and worldwide.&#x20;

In [popular music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_music), many English bands and solo artists have been cited as the most influential and best-selling musicians of all time. Acts such as [the Beatles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Beatles), [Led Zeppelin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Led_Zeppelin), [Pink Floyd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pink_Floyd), [Elton John](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elton_John), [Queen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_\(band\)), [Rod Stewart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rod_Stewart), [David Bowie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Bowie), [the Rolling Stones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Rolling_Stones) and [Def Leppard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Def_Leppard) are amongst the highest-selling recording artists in the world.[^cite_note-318] Many musical genres have origins in (or strong associations with) England, such as [British Invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Invasion), [progressive rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_rock), [hard rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard_rock), [Mod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mod_\(subculture\)), [glam rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glam_rock), [heavy metal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heavy_metal_music), [Britpop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Britpop), [indie rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indie_rock), [gothic rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_rock), [shoegazing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shoegaze), [acid house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acid_house), [garage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UK_garage), [trip hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trip_hop), [drum and bass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drum_and_bass) and [dubstep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dubstep).[^cite_note-319]&#x20;



The [Royal Albert Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Albert_Hall). Since the hall's opening in 1871, the world's leading artists from many performance genres have appeared on its stage.&#x20;

Large outdoor [music festivals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_music_festivals_in_the_United_Kingdom) in the summer and autumn are popular, such as [Glastonbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glastonbury_Festival), [V Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V_Festival), and the [Reading and Leeds Festivals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reading_and_Leeds_Festivals). England was at the forefront of the illegal, free [rave](43488.md) movement from the late 1980s, which inspired the pan-European culture of [teknivals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teknival).[^cite_note-320] The [Boishakhi Mela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boishakhi_Mela) is a [Bengali New Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_New_Year) festival celebrated by the [British Bangladeshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Bangladeshi) community. It is the largest open-air Asian festival in Europe. After the [Notting Hill Carnival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notting_Hill_Carnival), it is the second-largest street festival in the UK, attracting over 80,000 visitors.&#x20;

The most prominent [opera house](155040.md) in England is the [Royal Opera House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Opera_House) at [Covent Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covent_Garden).[^cite_note-foreman-321] [The Proms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Proms) is a major annual cultural event in the English calendar.[^cite_note-foreman-321] [The Royal Ballet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Royal_Ballet) is one of the world's foremost classical ballet companies. The [Royal Academy of Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Academy_of_Music) is the oldest [conservatoire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_school) in the UK, founded in 1822, receiving its [royal charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_charter) in 1830.[^cite_note-322] England is home to numerous major orchestras such as the [BBC Symphony Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_Symphony_Orchestra), the [Royal Philharmonic Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Philharmonic_Orchestra), the [Philharmonia Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philharmonia_Orchestra), and the [London Symphony Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Symphony_Orchestra).[^cite_note-323] Other forms of entertainment that originated in England include the [circus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circus)[^cite_note-324][^cite_note-325][^cite_note-326] and the [pantomime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pantomime).[^cite_note-Panto-327]&#x20;

### Cinema

Main article: [Cinema of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_the_United_Kingdom)
